### Analysis Studio: Product Design And Architecture — 101 to 110

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | Establish Analysis Studio as the central analysis workspace | One screen where any game or position Orbis can read — an engine game, an imported file, a finished broadcast, a puzzle position, a set-up position — can be stepped through, branched, asked of the engine, annotated and exported, without the original game ever being changed, and still useful with no engine installed. | partial | yes | src/renderer/src/components/chess/analysis/AnalysisStudio.tsx:53 is mounted at the 'study' area by src/renderer/src/components/chess/ChessView.tsx:191, and src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:122 keeps the game, the cursor, the branches and the engine's answers as four separate things; entry points reach it from Play (ChessView.tsx:174), Famous games (:184), Library (:188), Find a position (:198), Live (:200) and the position editor (:202); the no-engine and no-bridge refusals are in analysis/EnginePanel.tsx:66 and :80. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'the source game survives being explored, promoted, pruned and restored' (36/36 pass). | Three things are absent. Puzzle positions have no route in — ChessView.tsx:176 mounts PuzzleWorkspace with no study callback and nothing in src/renderer/src/components/chess/puzzles/ opens the studio. There is no export of any kind from the studio: no button, and no channel for it (src/preload/index.ts:799 exports only a game's PGN, and writePgn in src/shared/chess/pgn.ts:314 takes a flat move list, so variations and notes could not be written out anyway). And Find a position passes (gameId, ply) into ChessView.tsx:162 `study(id)`, which takes one argument, so the ply is dropped and a position found at move 30 opens at move 0. |
| 102 | Define the Analysis Studio workspace layout | A board-centred layout with the move list beside the board and the engine, evaluation history, notes and controls in secondary panels that can be collapsed; a compact arrangement for narrow screens and a roomier one for people who want several panels at once; and the chosen layout remembered for the profile. | partial | yes | analysis/analysis.css:8-38 is a board-side column plus a panel column; analysis.css:299 and :308 are container queries on the cw-screen container declared at chess.css:2, so the layout answers to the width of the screen beside an open side panel rather than the window; AnalysisStudio.tsx:303 keeps Moves, Engine and Report always reachable as a segmented control, so no action is behind a dead panel. | The three secondary panels are mutually exclusive tabs: there is no roomier layout that shows the move list, the engine and the report together, and nothing is collapsible — you cannot hide a panel to give the board more room, you can only swap which one is showing. Nothing is remembered: the panel choice and the board orientation are component state (AnalysisStudio.tsx:55-56) with no write to the profile or to localStorage, so both reset every time the screen is opened. No test covers the layout at any width. |
| 103 | Create a dedicated analysis session model | A session value holding an id, where the game came from, its original moves, the current position, the active branch, the notes, the engine's search state and whether it has been saved; UI actions must never be recorded as chess moves; and the session must survive navigation, an engine failure, a restart and a profile switch, or say plainly that the work is unsaved. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:84 defines AnalysisSession (game, tree, original, cursor, annotations, evaluations, undoStack, note, counter); the kind of source it is attached to is carried by game.source.kind (src/shared/chess/model.ts:19); opening a panel or turning the board is component state and never reaches the session, and analysis.ts:587 sessionProblems re-checks that the notes, the evaluations and the tree still agree. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'an instruction that points nowhere is ignored rather than acted on' and 'an evaluation is only recorded against the position it was calculated in' pass. | The session has no identifier, no persistence state and no engine-search state (that lives in the React hook, analysis/useEngineAnalysis.tsx:74). Nothing saves it: there is no chess study/analysis channel in src/preload/index.ts:777-804 and no store for one in src/main/chess/store.ts, so recovery after a restart or a profile switch is impossible. Worse, changing area in ChessView unmounts AnalysisStudio, so the tree, every note and every recorded evaluation are destroyed with no warning — the word 'unsaved' does not appear anywhere in the chess tree, so the section's persist-it-or-say-so requirement is met by neither half. |

The forward and end buttons are disabled when the node has no children (AnalysisStudio.tsx:285-286) so nothing is created implicitly, the move list jumps by node id (analysis/MoveTree.tsx:66), and 'Back to the main line' appears whenever the cursor is off it (AnalysisStudio.tsx:294). Tests: analysis.test.ts 'navigation walks the line and jumping lands where it is told' and 'entering, leaving and promoting a branch, and taking the promotion back' — both pass (36/36). | — |
| 105 | Separate source history from analysis history | The game as it was played must stay intact and recoverable while alternatives are explored, held as a real tree where each node knows its parent, the move and the position; the screen must show which line is the game and which is a branch; and promoting a branch must be an explicit act with a way back. | done | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:89 keeps `original` as the game's own node ids, fixed when the session opens; the tree is src/shared/chess/model.ts:99 (parentId, move, fen, children), never a notation string; analysis.ts:352 refuses to remove a move that is part of the game as played; analysis.ts:539 flattenLine works out main/nesting from the tree, and MoveTree.tsx:73 marks the played line 'played' even after another line has been promoted over it. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'the source game survives being explored, promoted, pruned and restored', 'the game as played cannot be pruned out of its own analysis', 'removing a line takes its whole subtree, and undo puts it back where it was', 'the move list tells the main line and the branches apart, and nests them' — all pass. | — |
| 106 | Design the engine-analysis control panel | A panel to start, pause, stop and configure a search, exposing time or node limits, depth, how many lines and the engine's own options; it must show the real search state — the position, elapsed time, progress — never call an intermediate depth a finished search, remember sensible preferences in the profile and offer to put them back. | partial | yes | analysis/EnginePanel.tsx:43 has start and stop, a time-or-depth choice, five thinking times, five depths and one to five lines; it shows the engine's status line, labels a bound as 'a bound, not a settled score', and when the board has moved on it says the numbers belong to a position we have left rather than relabelling them (:162). src/main/chess/ipc.ts:288 clamps every limit and refuses a search with no limit at all (engine.test.ts 'a search with no limit at all is refused rather than left to run for ever', 39/39 pass). | No pause. Nothing shows elapsed time, nodes or progress although the engine reports all three (AnalysisBase carries nodes/nps/timeMs, src/main/chess/engine.ts:135-137) — the panel renders only the score, the depth and the line. The panel's settings are local state (AnalysisStudio.tsx:57) with no write to the profile and no restore-defaults, while the profile already holds engine.limit/thinkMs/depth/lines/threads/hashMb (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:489-516) which the studio never reads. A stopped search still arrives as complete (engine.ts:533) with no incomplete label. And useEngineAnalysis.tsx:143 assumes the invoke resolves when the search finishes, but src/main/chess/ipc.ts:311 returns the request id at once, so after its 750 ms poll (useEngineAnalysis.tsx:146) the panel reports the engine as idle and disables Stop while Stockfish is still searching. |
| 107 | Support analysis of any valid supported game | Any game whose moves can be replayed must be analysable — club games, school games, your own — without needing ratings or a famous name; missing metadata must be shown as missing rather than invented; and a game that becomes illegal must name the move and position where it failed and offer the legal part. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:128-141 replays the stored moves and, when one cannot be played, keeps the prefix and sets a note naming the move number and the move ('This game stops being playable at move N'), which AnalysisStudio.tsx:313 shows; nothing filters on rating or player name anywhere in the studio. src/shared/chess/pgn.ts:287-299 gives an exact reason for an illegal move ('Move 14… "Qxf7" cannot be played in the position it reaches. Unknown players: library.test.ts 'Ada vs an unnamed player' (40/40 pass). | The failure half is untested. analysis.test.ts only asserts note === null for a sound game (line 52); the unplayable-prefix branch has no test, and src/shared/chess/pgn.ts has no test file of its own — readPgn is only touched by variants.test.ts for variant detection — so the 'precise, recoverable error message' requirement rests on unexercised code. The studio also never offers the choice the section asks for: it silently analyses the prefix rather than asking. |
| 108 | Implement analysis profiles | Named profiles — Quick Review, Balanced Analysis, Deep Analysis, Custom — each mapping to real engine settings and resource limits rather than a label, with the active profile recorded alongside the analysis and the honest caveat that depth depends on the machine and the position. | not started | no | Nothing in the tree: grep across src for 'Quick Review', 'Balanced Analysis', 'Deep Analysis', 'analysisProfile' and 'ANALYSIS_PROFILES' returns no hit in any chess file. The only search configuration is the ad-hoc time/depth/lines in analysis/EnginePanel.tsx:28 and the hard-coded sweep budget at AnalysisStudio.tsx:174-182. | There are no profiles at all — no named presets, no mapping from a preset to engine settings and resource limits, nothing recorded with an analysis to say which preset produced it, and no wording about depth depending on hardware and position. |
| 109 | Support whole-game analysis jobs | A way to analyse a whole game in one go, within a bounded budget, that can be cancelled, keeps what it already finished so an interrupted run can resume, distinguishes a run that covered everything asked from one that stopped early, and documents how it chose which positions to search deeply rather than hiding what it skipped. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:1044 planQuickPass shortens the time per position before it gives anything up, then gives up the opening rather than striding through the game (which would name no move at all), and lists what it dropped; analysis.ts:1138 planDeeper picks the moves where the numbers moved and asks for both positions of each because that is what naming a move takes; analysis.ts:1211 planCoverage compares asked against answered. AnalysisStudio.tsx:171 runs the two passes, shows the plan's own notes and the count done, and cancels (:249). Tests: analysis.test.ts 'a plan that fits covers every position, in order, and says nothing was skipped', 'a tight budget shortens the time per position before it gives anything up', 'a budget that cannot cover the game keeps a run to the end, and lists what it dropped', 'the deeper pass picks the moves where the numbers moved, and asks for both their positions', 'coverage compares what was asked with what came back' — all pass. | Nothing is kept: the results live in React state, so an interrupted sweep cannot be resumed and everything it found is destroyed when the area changes. The run itself is also broken in a way no test can see, because runSweep has no test: it waits at most 750 ms for each position (useEngineAnalysis.tsx:146) while the deep pass asks for up to 3000 ms (AnalysisStudio.tsx:178), so deep searches are recorded at whatever shallow depth had arrived or not recorded at all, and because src/main/chess/ipc.ts:311 returns immediately the next request queues behind a search still running (engine.ts:1140). |

Tests: analysis.test.ts 'a report built from the session uses the evaluations recorded on the game s own nodes' plus the classification and critical-moment tests — all pass. | No save and no export: the report is recomputed from the session on every render (AnalysisStudio.tsx:79) and there is no channel to store it, so it cannot be reopened later. The game's result is also absent from the panel. |

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 111 | Define the evaluation perspective consistently | One documented internal convention for engine scores, converted only at defined boundaries, with the favoured side always clear and every dependent display — bar, number, variation list, graph, report — moving together when the perspective changes; centipawn and mate scores must be separate types with no silent arithmetic between them. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/evaluation.ts:9-11 states the convention (always White-positive), evaluation.ts:24 fromEngine turns the engine's side-to-move number round exactly once at the boundary, and :30 forSide converts for a reader; cp and mate are separate variants of Score (:14) and evaluation.ts:65 lossFrom returns null rather than inventing a centipawn figure across a mate. Tests: engine.test.ts 'a mate score is a mate, and it is turned round for the side to move exactly once' (39/39 pass); analysis.test.ts 'loss and classification are read from the mover s side, not White s'; settings.test.ts formatForSettings perspective assertions (58/58 pass). | The perspective preference does not reach the analysis screen. src/shared/chess/settings.ts:524 stores analysis.from ('Always White' or 'Whoever is to move') and settings.ts:949 formatForSettings honours it, but AnalysisStudio, EnginePanel, MoveTree and ReportPanel all call the raw formatScore, so switching the preference changes the settings preview and nothing else — the numbers in the studio stay White-positive, which is the one inconsistency the section's acceptance criteria names. |
| 112 | Build an accurate evaluation bar | A bar showing the current advantage with a stable convention and a readable number, updating only when a valid result arrives for the position actually on the board, labelling any retained figure as belonging to an earlier search, hideable by preference, and never the only way to read the number. | partial | yes | analysis/EvalGraph.tsx:16 EvalBar is a vertical bar that turns round with the board, carries the number as text, and renders an explicit unknown state ('—', aria-label 'No evaluation for this position') rather than a zero. AnalysisStudio.tsx:94-96 only takes a live engine line whose positionKey matches the board and otherwise falls back to this node's own recorded figure, so a number from another position can never reach it; the same figure is also in the move list (MoveTree.tsx:71) and the report, so the bar is not the only route to it. | There is no working preference to hide it. src/shared/chess/settings.ts:538 stores analysis.evalBar and its own field record says 'Nothing draws the bar yet', so the switch is stored and read by nothing — turning it off leaves the bar on screen. The bar itself has no test, and barFraction's mapping (evaluation.ts:48 — mate pinned to the end of the bar, the cp curve) is asserted nowhere; analysis.test.ts:285 only checks that a fraction is null at a gap and non-null elsewhere. |
| 113 | Display centipawn and mate evaluations | Centipawn scores formatted consistently with a sign and an agreed precision, mate scores shown as mate information rather than a huge decimal, the mate distance explained in the engine's own terms without over-claiming, and an explicit unavailable state instead of zero — consistently across Chess World. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/evaluation.ts:36 formatScore gives '+1.24', 'M3', 'M-2' and '—' for no score, never '0.00' for a missing one; src/shared/chess/settings.ts:949 formatForSettings adds the pawns/hundredths choice, the perspective choice and 'mate in 3' in words, and refuses to let the unit touch a mate. Tests: settings.test.ts 'a forced mate is spelt out or abbreviated, and is never turned into a number of pawns' and the '—' assertion at settings.test.ts:564 — 58/58 pass. | Two formatters, and the studio uses the one that ignores the profile: EnginePanel.tsx, MoveTree.tsx and ReportPanel.tsx all call formatScore, so the person's chosen unit, perspective and mate wording apply in the settings screen and nowhere else — formatting is therefore not consistent throughout Chess World, which is exactly this section's acceptance criteria. What a mate distance means (moves, not plies, per the engine's convention) is documented in code at evaluation.ts:16 but never said on screen. |
| 114 | Implement meaningful move classification | Categories such as best, excellent, good, inaccuracy, mistake, blunder and missed opportunity, derived from written-down rules over real engine evaluations, with mate results treated differently from ordinary centipawn changes, adjustable only through defined options, and an uncertain state whenever the search is too shallow to support a name. | done | yes | src/shared/chess/evaluation.ts:80 CLASS_THRESHOLDS writes the boundaries down (20/50/100/250) and :82 classify returns 'unclear' below minDepth 8, names 'missed-mate' when a forced mate was available and not taken, calls walking into a mate a blunder, and refuses a centipawn verdict when a mate score sits on only one side; src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:925 explainClass says in words what each name rests on, including 'Searched only N deep, which is not enough to name this move'. On screen as marks in MoveTree.tsx:13 (blank for an unjudged move) and counts in ReportPanel.tsx:79. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'a shallow search names nothing, and the depth behind a judgement is the shallower of the two', 'a mate that was there and was not played is reported as that, never as a centipawn loss', 'loss and classification are read from the mover s side, not White s' — all pass. | — |
| 115 | Compare the played move with engine alternatives | For each analysed move, show the position before it, what was played, what the engine recommended and what changed, with enough of the principal variation to explain the idea and a way to try the alternative — and no claim that a move lost material unless the line shows it. | partial | no | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:799-836 builds a row per move holding the position before it, the score before and after, the loss from the mover's side, the class and — only when the engine named a different move and that move is legal in the position — `better: { uci, san }`; the recommendation's own line is kept as pvUci on the evaluation (analysis.ts:61) and analysis.ts:1389 writeLine can write it out against the position it was calculated in. No wording claims lost material: explainClass reports pawns given up and the depth behind it. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'the report names a better move only when the engine named a different one' and 'a line is written out against the position it was calculated in' — both pass. | None of it is on screen. analysis/ReportPanel.tsx never renders report.moves — it shows the summary, the figures, the counts, the critical moments, the missed mates and the unused numbers — so the played-versus-recommended comparison, the recommendation's principal variation and any way to explore the alternative do not exist in the interface. The data is computed and tested and then thrown away unseen. |
| 116 | Detect candidate critical moments | Pick out the positions worth reviewing from real evidence — big evaluation changes, tactical swings, missed forced wins, defensive decisions — using configurable thresholds and only where the search is complete enough, and store with each finding the position, the move number, the evaluation context, why it was chosen and the analysis configuration behind it. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:937 criticalFor raises missed-mate, walked-into-mate, blunder, mistake and swing — and a swing only when the score crosses level by at least swingCp, not on any large number; analysis.ts:861 refuses to look for a moment at all unless the search is deep enough and neither score is a bound; the moments are ranked by severity, one per move, and the ones beyond the cap are counted as moreCritical rather than dropped silently (:894). The thresholds (swingCp, maxCritical, minDepth) are ReportOptions with no control on screen. And because nothing persists the report, no finding is stored at all — it is recomputed on every render. |

Trying an alternative from that position always becomes a branch and never edits the game (analysis.ts:277 addMove, tested by 'the source game survives being explored, promoted, pruned and restored'). Test: analysis.test.ts 'walking into a mate is a critical moment of its own, and mate scores read per side' passes. | There is no defensive analysis as such. | 119 | Build an evaluation graph | A graph of the evaluation across the game with a clear move axis and evaluation axis, handling mate scores and wide ranges without flattening ordinary changes; every point must come from a real recorded result, selecting one must move the board and move list with it, and unanalysed positions must show as gaps rather than invented values. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:1418 graphSegments returns broken runs precisely so no line is drawn across a stretch nobody searched, and :1439 graphGaps names the gaps; analysis/EvalGraph.tsx:64 shades them and the caption says which plies they are; a lone analysed point is drawn as a dot (:77) so it cannot vanish; mate is pinned to the ends of the scale by evaluation.ts:48 so one forced mate does not flatten the rest; clicking picks a ply (:47) which moves the board, the move list, the bar and the notes together through one cursor. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'nothing is drawn across a gap: the graph comes back as separate runs' and 'a position that was not analysed is a gap in the graph and an unnamed move in the list' — both pass. | There are no axes. The graph has a centre line and nothing else — no move numbers along the bottom, no evaluation scale up the side, no ticks or labels — so a point's value can only be guessed from its height. And 'incomplete' is only visible as a gap: a position searched to depth 4 is drawn exactly like one searched to depth 30, even though GraphPoint carries the depth and the report counts the shallow ones separately. |
| 120 | Create a post-game performance summary | Summarise the game from what was actually measured — move classifications, big swings, tactical chances, the themes the review found — without reducing play to one unexplained accuracy percentage, and offer specific next steps drawn from the real positions rather than generic filler. | partial | yes | analysis/ReportPanel.tsx:58-87 shows positions analysed out of the total, how many were too shallow to judge and under what depth, the average centipawns given up per side with the number of moves that average rests on, the counts by class, and how many moves are deliberately not named; src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:974 summarise says all of this in a sentence including what is missing. There is no bare accuracy figure on the panel, and where an accuracy metric does exist it ships its own definition and limits: src/shared/chess/progress.ts:504 ACCURACY_FORMULA and :508 four notes, including that it is not what any other program calls accuracy. | No themes and no next steps. Nothing in the report names a pattern to review or an endgame to practise from the positions in this game. The machinery for it exists and is disconnected at both ends: src/shared/chess/coach.ts:728 weaknessProfile and :824 suggestPlan work over an AnalysedGame record (coach.ts:135) that nothing in the tree ever builds from a report, and ChessView.tsx:186 mounts CoachPanel with only `played` and no evidence, so no analysis of any game has ever reached a summary or a plan. |

### Variation Explorer — 121 to 130

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 121 | Create a full variation-tree workspace | Let people branch from any valid position, branch again inside a branch, move between branches and compare where they lead, all without touching the source game; the analysis must be a real tree of positions and moves, each node able to rebuild its board and name its parent, and the screen must show which branch you are in and how it relates to the main line. | done | yes | src/shared/chess/model.ts:99 VariationNode holds parentId, the move, the resulting position and its children, and model.ts:282 treeProblems proves there are no loops, no orphans and no move that cannot be played from its parent; src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:190 branchPoint gives a node's relationship to the main line, :249/:256 step into and back out of a branch, and :539 flattenLine hands the screen nesting, main, startsBranch and closes so MoveTree.tsx:57-74 can indent and bracket nested lines and say 'variation, N deep' to a screen reader. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'the move list tells the main line and the branches apart, and nests them', 'promoting pulls every move of the line up, not only its last one', 'the source game survives being explored, promoted, pruned and restored' — all pass. It lives inside Analysis Studio rather than as a separate screen, which this section does not require. | — |
| 122 | Create a variation from any position | From any selected move or position, a clear way to start a new branch; a legal move played on the board joins the selected branch, an illegal one is refused without changing anything, the parent position is always identified, there is an obvious way back to the main line, and a move played from a historical position must never be appended to the end of the game. | done | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:277 addMove plays from the cursor's own position, returns null for an illegal move so nothing changes, and reuses an existing branch instead of making a second copy of the same idea; AnalysisStudio.tsx:107 only accepts a board move when SESSION_RIGHTS.analysis.variations allows it (src/shared/chess/model.ts:90) and then says in words 'added as a line. The game itself is unchanged'; the way back is prev and 'Back to the main line' (AnalysisStudio.tsx:284, :295). Because the move is always applied to the cursor's position, a move from move 12 branches at move 12 and cannot reach the end of the game. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'a move that cannot be played changes nothing, and a move already there is reused' and 'entering, leaving and promoting a branch, and taking the promotion back' — both pass. | — |
| 123 | Display candidate variations from Stockfish | Ask Stockfish for several candidate lines where the engine supports it and show each with its evaluation and search context; the lines must belong to the exact position and request that produced them and must not be presented against a position the board has left; and a candidate line must be openable in the variation tree, with its moves validated and without pointlessly duplicating an existing branch. | partial | yes | One to five lines are requested and clamped (EnginePanel.tsx:131, src/main/chess/ipc.ts:307) and the engine clamps again to what it advertises; each line is shown with its score, its depth, and its moves written out against the position it was calculated in, plus 'a bound, not a settled score' when the engine only gave a bound (EnginePanel.tsx:169-178). src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:1333 acceptUpdate drops any answer whose request id or position does not match and counts what it dropped, which the panel shows (:191), and when the board has moved on the numbers are labelled as belonging to a position we have left. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'an answer is taken only when both the request and the position match', 'each line keeps its own slot, the latest answer replacing the one before it', 'a line is written out against the position it was calculated in' — all pass. | A candidate line cannot be opened in the variation tree. There is no function that walks a pvUci into the session — analysis.ts has addMove for one move at a time and writeLine for display only — and no control for it in EnginePanel, so the engine's line can be read and never explored. |
| 124 | Compare candidate moves | A comparison view for selected candidate moves showing the starting position, each candidate, its evaluation and a meaningful continuation; scores must be on one perspective and genuinely comparable, differing search budgets must be shown as context, and no candidate should be declared best while the evidence is incomplete. | partial | yes | The multi-line list in EnginePanel.tsx:169 does show the candidates from one position with their scores, their depths and their continuations, all White-positive (src/shared/chess/evaluation.ts:9) and all from a single search, so they are on one scale and one budget; a bounded score is marked unsettled and a line that cannot be played out to the end says so (:176-177), so nothing is declared superior on incomplete evidence. | There is no comparison view. Nothing shows the position each candidate leads to, there is no side-by-side, there is no way to choose which candidates to compare, and nothing carries a per-candidate search budget for the case the section is actually about — candidates from different searches — even though the engine reports nodes, nps and time for each (src/main/chess/engine.ts:135-137) and EngineLine keeps them (analysis.ts:1292). What exists is a list from one search, not a comparison. |
| 125 | Promote a variation to the main line | Let a branch be adopted as the primary line of a saved analysis or study as a deliberate act distinct from merely viewing it; keep enough to recover the previous version before changing a saved main line, keep annotations on their correct positions, report metadata that cannot be mapped safely, and never silently overwrite an imported game. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:312 promote lifts every move on the way down (not just the last) and records each child's previous index, so analysis.ts:467 undo restores the exact former order rather than re-deriving it; it is its own control, 'Make this the main line' (AnalysisStudio.tsx:319), separate from clicking the move to view it, and canPromote disables it when the line already is the main line. Notes are keyed by node (analysis.ts:418) so they stay on their own moves, and nothing in the module ever returns a session with a different game, so the imported record is untouched and the game as played stays in the list marked 'played'. Tests: analysis.test.ts 'entering, leaving and promoting a branch, and taking the promotion back', 'promoting pulls every move of the line up, not only its last one', 'a note is attached to a node and follows it through promotion', 'the source game survives being explored, promoted, pruned and restored' — all pass. | There is no saved analysis or study for a promotion to be adopted into: nothing persists a session (no channel in src/preload/index.ts:777-804, nothing in src/main/chess/store.ts), so a promoted main line exists only while the screen is open and is destroyed when the area changes, and 'recover the previous version' reaches no further than the open screen's own undo stack. Nothing reports a metadata conflict either — there is no code path for it. |
| 126 | Rename and organise variations | Let a person put a descriptive name on an important branch ("King-side attack"), keep many branches organised without an arbitrary cap, and have those names still be on the right branch when the study is reopened. | not started | no | src/shared/chess/model.ts:107 declares `label?: string` on VariationNode and nothing anywhere writes or reads it. | No function and no control sets a branch label; nothing sorts or groups branches. There is also nowhere for a label to persist: src/renderer/src/components/chess/analysis/AnalysisStudio.tsx:62 rebuilds the whole session with `openSession(game)` every time the screen opens, and no IPC channel saves a study (src/preload/index.ts has no study channel), so "persist locally and remain associated after reopening" cannot hold. Two undocumented caps exist in src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:539 — a 20,000-row limit and `nesting >= 40`, which silently skips deeper branches with no stated performance reason. |
| 127 | Annotate branches and positions | Let a person write notes and move marks on positions and moves, keyed to the position rather than to a row number, kept clearly apart from the imported file's own comments and the engine's words, and editable, deletable and recoverable. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:418 `annotate`, :430 `clearAnnotation`, :403 `annotationOf` all key by node id; :408 `sourceComment` keeps the PGN's own comment separate; undo of an annotation is in the Reversible union at :72. src/renderer/src/components/chess/analysis/Notes.tsx:68 shows the file's comment under a "From the file" tag beside the person's own. Tests pass: src/shared/chess/analysis.test.ts:199 'a note is attached to a node and follows it through promotion' and :214 'a note follows its node through a removal and a restore, and an empty note is no note' (36/36 pass). | Notes live only in React state and are thrown away when the screen closes — there is no save path at all, so "editing, deletion and recovery consistent with ORBIS's data-handling conventions" is only true within one sitting. Notes attach to nodes only; there is no note on a branch as a whole. No formatting of any kind (plain textarea). Engine text is in other panels but is never labelled as a third kind of comment beside the two in the notes box. |
| 128 | Compare end positions | Let a person pick two variations and see the positions they end in side by side, with evaluations, and be told honestly when two boards that look alike are not the same position because the side to move, castling rights or en-passant square differ. | not started | no | No comparison exists. Only src/shared/chess/analysis.ts and src/shared/chess/model.ts touch the variation tree at all; neither has a compare function, and src/renderer/src/components/chess/analysis/ has no compare view. | Nothing selects two variations, nothing shows two end positions together, nothing reports their evaluations side by side. The state-aware comparison the section is mostly about already exists and is tested — src/shared/chess/openings.ts:376 `transposition` reports placement/turn/castling/en-passant differences in plain words, covered by src/shared/chess/openings.test.ts:152 and :173 — but it is reachable only from the Opening Lab and is never applied to a variation tree. |
| 129 | Export a study with variations | Export a saved study as PGN with its branches, their order and their comments intact, and say plainly what will be left out rather than claiming a lossless export. | not started | no | src/shared/chess/pgn.ts:314 `writePgn` writes only `game.moves` — one flat token stream, no parentheses, no variation nesting, and nothing from the session's annotations. The reader at :172-173 deliberately skips everything inside brackets. The only export channels are `chess:export-pgn` and `chess:to-pgn` (src/main/chess/ipc.ts:141, :159), both of which export the stored game. | There is no study to export (studies are never saved) and no export control in the Analysis Studio. The PGN writer cannot emit variations, annotations or NAGs that a session holds, and nothing discloses the omission. Writing variations out would need a new tree-to-PGN writer plus a matching reader. |
| 130 | Maintain variation-tree integrity | Check every branch edit against its parent position, keep loops, orphans and illegal moves out of the tree, never let deleting a branch take the source game with it, and check a saved study when it is loaded, recovering the sound branches and naming the damaged part. | partial | — | src/shared/chess/model.ts:282 `treeProblems` detects loops, missing parents, orphans and moves illegal from their parent; :264 `removeBranch` prunes the subtree and cleans every dangling child reference. src/shared/chess/analysis.ts:277 `addMove` plays the move through the rules before adding it; :352 `removeLine` refuses to remove a node of the game as played and says to use the Library instead; :587 `sessionProblems` also cross-checks notes and evaluations against the tree. Tests pass: analysis.test.ts:97 'a move that cannot be played changes nothing', :166 'the game as played cannot be pruned out of its own analysis', :176 'removing a line takes its whole subtree, and undo puts it back where it was'. | The load-and-recover half is absent because no study is ever persisted — `openSession` always rebuilds from the stored game, so there is no saved tree to check, no damaged portion to name and no partial recovery. `sessionProblems` is never shown anywhere on screen. And detection itself is untested: every assertion in analysis.test.ts (:58, :94, :149, :194, :224) checks `sessionProblems` is empty, so no test ever feeds in a corrupt tree and confirms the problems are found; `treeProblems` has no test of its own. |

Separately, large imports do block: `importPgn` awaits `Collection.put` once per game and each put rebuilds the whole array, so an n-game import into an n-game library is quadratic work on the main process (`Collection.replace`/`update` exist precisely to avoid this and are not used). readPgn's multi-game and failure paths have no test — the only coverage is two variant cases in variants.test.ts. |

Tests pass in library.test.ts: 'the same moves in two different tournaments are not one game', 'duplicate groups keep the three kinds apart', 'two empty boards are not duplicates of each other'. | The import path uses none of that. `ChessStore.importPgn` compares the fingerprint alone — start position plus moves — and with the default `'skip'` policy discards any incoming game whose moves already exist, which is exactly the 'same moves, different game' case the comparison exists to refuse. `DuplicatePolicy` ('skip' \| 'keep-both') is a parameter of importPgn that neither `chess:import-pgn` nor `chess:import-pgn-text` ever passes, so keep-both and a review step are unreachable from the window; the only trace of a skipped record is a bare count in the summary. |

Tests pass in library.test.ts: 'tags and collections are kept apart, and a name that would break that is refused', 'a bulk tag is worked out per game rather than written over the top'. | Rename is absent. Neither a tag nor a collection can be renamed: library.ts has no rename helper, TagSheet only adds and removes, and the only way to change a name is to take the old one off every game and put a new one on. ### Data Integrity, Security And Privacy — 191 to 200

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 191 | Validate all imported chess data | Treat PGN files, FEN strings, database files and game events arriving from outside as untrusted: check the format, hold them to sensible size limits, cope with rubbish without crashing, and never run code out of a file or trust its metadata as configuration. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/pgn.ts:57 PGN_LIMITS caps a file at 64 MB, 20,000 games, 2,000 moves and 20,000 comment characters, and reading faults are kept apart from playing faults with every failure listed by index and reason. Every FEN that enters is checked (src/main/chess/ipc.ts:88 and :255 via `checkFen`), and src/main/chess/store.ts:38 replays stored moves through rules.ts rather than trusting them, so a hand-edited file cannot put an illegal game back. Every IPC handler takes `unknown` and clamps (src/main/chess/ipc.ts:58-74). Archive entries are refused before a byte is written. Nothing executes from a file: the engine path only ever comes from an option or the environment (src/main/chess/install.ts:423). Passing tests: src/shared/chess/rules.test.ts rejects seven kinds of broken FEN by name; src/shared/chess/variants.test.ts 'the importer read a Chess960 game as if it were chess' refuses an unsupported variant and builds not one move of it; src/shared/portable/zip.test.ts refuses absolute paths, backslashes, null bytes, over-long paths, duplicate paths and a decompression bomb. | The PGN size and count limits themselves are never exercised — no test feeds readPgn an oversized file, a 3,000-move game or a truncated game — and src/main/chess/store.ts:147 importPgn and the `chess:import-pgn` channel have no test at all, so 'oversized inputs are rejected or handled safely' is proved for FEN, variants and archives but not for the PGN path a person actually uses. |

Shown on screen in editor/VariantNotice.tsx:70-77 (expandable list of all refused variants with what each can do and how it differs), reached from Chess World's 'Set up a position' area. Tested: variants.test.ts 'every refused variant says what makes it a different game' and the refusal-wording assertions at :70-74 (44/44 pass). The explanations are accurate by construction — they explain why Orbis refuses, which is exactly what the validator does. | The instructions are not displayed before a first game and are not available during play; they live only in the position-editor screen and in refusal messages. There is no variant a person can select and play, so no in-play rules panel exists. |
| 257 | Keep variant ratings separate | Keep any variant performance rating in its own history, apart from the standard-chess one, and never present a locally computed training score as an official rating. | done | yes | No variant can be played (variants.test.ts:48 pins the playable set to standard), so no variant score can reach standard statistics. The honesty requirement is implemented and tested: src/shared/chess/puzzleRating.ts:18,112,289 keep a figure below ATTEMPTS_FOR_A_RATING a 'working figure, not a rating', shown with the attempt count and never alone, and :397 says a movement is 'how hard it has been for you, not a rating from many solvers'; src/shared/chess/arena.ts:49 'Strength is not a rating'. Tests pass: puzzleRating.test.ts 'one attempt is not a rating' and 'still not a rating at N attempts' (16/16); coach.test.ts 'it says out loud that it is not a rating and not about how you think'. Visible on the Puzzles screen. | — |
| 258 | Support variant-aware imports | On import, detect the variant from reliable file metadata or an explicit choice by the user, validate the moves with the matching rules, ask rather than guess when the variant is unclear, and when it is unsupported keep the source record intact and report the limitation. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/pgn.ts:264-281 reads the Variant tag, resolves it through the one shared list of standard spellings, and on anything else records problem 'unsupported-variant' with a reason naming the game — reading no moves, so the source is never half-interpreted. src/main/chess/store.ts:152-161 collects those into ImportSummary.failed with the index, reason and player names and stores nothing; the file on disk is untouched. Shown on screen in library/ImportSheet.tsx:93-109. Tests pass: variants.test.ts 'a real Chess960 file is refused by the importer and still refused by the registry', 'a game written as "From Position" is ordinary chess and is read', 'every spelling the importer treats as ordinary chess is ordinary chess here too' (44/44). | The user is never asked. An unrecognised Variant tag — the case the section says to ask about rather than guess — is refused outright (variants.ts:334-336), and there is no way for a person to state the variant on import; `chessCreate` accepts a variant field but no caller in the UI passes one. |
| 259 | Design future variant extension points | Keep ruleset-specific behaviour behind documented interfaces so a future variant can bring its own starting position, legal-move rules, termination conditions, notation and engine capabilities — without overgeneralising the standard-chess code or making it hard to test. | partial | — | The capability side is a real, documented extension point: adding a VariantRecord to src/shared/chess/variants.ts:79 needs no change to any other module, the alias index is built from the data (:270-277), and the invariants are tested (variants.test.ts 'no two variants claim the same name', 'every variant Orbis knows and refuses is refused'; 44/44 pass). The module's own header (:7-30) documents the boundary and states that it is not a second set of rules — check is asked of rules.ts rather than recomputed. | Only the capability metadata is behind an interface. There is no ruleset interface for the things the section lists: rules.ts is a single hardcoded standard-chess implementation, nothing in the tree takes a ruleset parameter, and the engine, notation and termination paths have no variant seam. Adding a playable variant would mean threading a new ruleset through rules.ts, the analysis path, the arena and pgn.ts. |
| 260 | Establish variant release gates | Before a variant is released, run its own tests for starting positions, legal moves, special rules, endings, persistence, imports, exports and engine integration, rather than leaning on standard-chess regression tests. | done | — | The gate exists and is enforced by a passing test: src/shared/chess/variants.test.ts:48 asserts `playableVariants()` is exactly ['standard'] and :49-55 that no other record claims play, analyse, import or export — so no variant can become available without that test being changed deliberately. Every refusal path has its own test (:58,80,99,108,139,149,188), and variants.test.ts is in the runner list at scripts/testFiles.mjs:255. Confirmed passing: 44/44. | — |

| 261 | Treat 3D chess as an optional presentation mode | Offer a 3D board as an extra that never replaces the flat one, driven by the same game state and move validation, so a person can go back to 2D without losing the game, variation, analysis session or position. | done | no | src/renderer/src/components/chess/three/Board3D.tsx:57-76 takes exactly the flat board's props and keeps no position or history of its own; :1-55 states the rule and :700-712 renders the real `Board` component for the flat route. three/Board3DArea.tsx:28-80 derives both views from one `fen` and one `ply`, and carries the switch both ways. selection.ts:1-10 is the whole of the 3D board's opinion about chess and it defers to rules.ts. Tests pass: selection.test.ts (26), scene.test.ts (18/18 run alone), support.test.ts, camera.test.ts, boardGeometry.test.ts — 58/58 for the first four files; all eight are in scripts/testFiles.mjs:261-268. | — |
| 262 | Define the rendering technology boundary | Pick a rendering approach that fits the existing desktop runtime, reuse what is already there, and keep 3D rendering away from chess rules, storage, engine execution and live feeds so a graphics failure cannot kill Stockfish or damage a saved game. | done | — | three.js was already a dependency for the Orbis Files model viewer (package.json:119, src/files/renderer/features/viewers/ModelViewer.tsx), and Board3D.tsx:50-55 documents reusing it with no new licence. It is loaded on demand inside a try/catch that routes to the flat board: Board3D.tsx:286-298 sets trouble 'no-library'. three/scene.ts:6,29 has no static import of three at all — the library is passed in — which is why the scene is testable without a GPU. All 3D code is renderer-side while the engine runs in the main process (src/main/chess/engine.ts), so the processes cannot take each other down. Tests pass: support.test.ts 'every way the 3D board can fail falls back to the flat one and says so plainly'; scene.test.ts 'a scene that cannot be built does not leave half of one behind' and 'taking the scene down lets go of every shape and every material'. | — |
| 263 | Build a readable 3D chessboard | Make squares, pieces, coordinates, selection and move highlights clearly tellable apart, with lighting and materials that keep pieces recognisable rather than decorative, sensible camera defaults, nothing obscuring legal-move marks, and readability at the supported window sizes and display scales. | done | no | three/pieces.ts builds the men as turned profiles; tests pass: 'the six pieces are six clearly different heights', 'the knight is the one piece that is not the same from every side', 'the king is the only piece with a cross and the queen the only one with a ring of beads', 'no piece leaves its own square'. three/palette.ts:148-162 measures piece-against-square separation by fill or foot band; palette.test.ts 'every piece stands at least three to one against every square' and 'the two square colours are clearly different from each other'. Coordinates: boardGeometry.ts:130-142 places them outside the squares and follows the board round ('the coordinates sit outside the squares and follow the board round'); scene.test.ts 'the coordinates can be hidden and brought back'. Camera defaults and clamps: camera.ts:35-49,79-99. Display scale: support.ts:112-115 caps device pixel ratio, with scene.test.ts 'the viewport is only taken when it is a real size'. | — |
| 264 | Implement camera controls | Give intuitive rotation, zoom and a way back to the default orientation, using inputs that do not clash with existing ORBIS shortcuts or normal window use, and make sure moving the camera can never change the position or play a move. | done | no | three/camera.ts:102-139 (drag rotate, whole-step key rotate, proportional zoom, device-independent wheel steps), :79-99 (default view plus named overhead and side views for the reset control), :65-71 clamps so the camera can never leave the usable range. Camera state is three numbers and touches no chess state. three/interaction.ts separates looking from playing. Tests pass: camera.test.ts 'the view can never leave the limits, whatever it is handed', 'the camera stays above the board at every angle it is allowed to reach', 'from the limits of the tilt, the middle of the board is still clickable', 'swinging back to a view takes the short way round'; interaction.test.ts 'once a press has travelled far enough it is a look, and it can never go back', 'the right button looks and never plays', 'the wheel zooms only where it will not take the page's scrolling away', 'the application keeps every shortcut it had'. Reset and named views are wired to buttons in Board3D.tsx:741-752. | — |

Tests pass: boardGeometry.test.ts 'a line from the camera finds the square it is pointing at', 'a line that cannot reach the board says so instead of inventing a square', 'a point off the board belongs to no square'; selection.test.ts 'every square the board offers is a square the rules offered, and no others', 'no illegal move can get through the board, from any square to any square', 'no legal move in any opening position is ever refused by the board', 'a square the piece cannot go to lets go and plays nothing', 'a pawn reaching the last rank asks what it becomes before anything is played'. | — |

All of side-to-move, promotion, castling and en passant therefore come from the shared FEN. Tests pass: scene.test.ts 'showing a different position moves the pieces that are there rather than building more' and 'turning the board round moves the pieces and nothing else'; selection.test.ts 'the marks come from the rules and from nothing else'. | No test exercises the switch itself, and Board3DArea.tsx — the component that actually holds the shared ply, orientation and loose-move list for both views, and so is the only place navigation could desynchronise — has no test file at all. |

Applied by document attribute so they reach every board in the window (settings.ts:889-902 and settings/chessSettings.css:30-105), and picked in Chess World's Settings area. Tested, passing (58/58): settings.test.ts 'every theme has a visible difference between its two squares', 'every piece can be seen on both of its squares, by its colour or by its outline', 'the stylesheet and the colour data are the same colours', 'the check square is painted above the square colour, not under it'. No licence exposure anywhere: the flat pieces are Unicode glyphs (Board.tsx:19-23 'so no image files are needed and nothing is licensed') and the 3D pieces are generated geometry, so there is no bundled artwork to record provenance for. | The 3D board has no theme choice and ignores the chosen one. three/palette.ts:14-15,46-60 hardcodes Slate's two square colours into DEFAULT_PALETTE and readPalette (:93-101) takes only the accent, frame and text from the page — so selecting Walnut or High contrast changes the flat board and leaves the 3D board slate-coloured. This section sits in the 3D part of the spec, where no selectable theme set exists. |
| 268 | Add optional move animations | Offer configurable movement and capture animations that read clearly without slowing play, interruptible when a person navigates quickly or switches views, and never let an animation delay the logical move, the engine update or legal-move validation. | partial | no | The correctness half is right and tested: the move is committed by rules.ts before anything is drawn (selection.ts:75-78 returns the rules' own move; scene.ts follows the resulting state), so an animation cannot cause a duplicate move or a stale position. What animation exists honours reduced motion: support.ts:95-104 (resetDuration 260ms, liftDuration 130ms, both zero when less movement is asked for), driven from Board3D.tsx:82-86,435,603. Tests pass: support.test.ts 'a machine that has asked for less movement gets none', 'a piece lifts far enough to be obvious and not far enough to leave its square'; scene.test.ts 'a held piece rises off its square and letting go puts it back', 'a lift survives the position changing under it'. | There is no movement or capture animation at all — a piece jumps straight to its new square, because scene.setPosition simply repositions the pieces (scene.ts:402-410) and nothing tweens between squares. There is also no in-app animation setting: the only control is the operating system's reduced-motion preference, so 'configurable' is unmet. |
| 269 | Enforce adaptive rendering performance | Measure rendering performance on representative devices and cut detail — simpler materials, fewer effects, lower level of detail, or a switch to 2D — when the 3D view cannot hold an acceptable frame rate, while not burning CPU or GPU when the view is hidden or idle. | partial | no | The idle and hidden cases are properly handled and tested: support.ts:82-86 shouldDraw refuses a frame unless the canvas is on screen, the window is the one being looked at, and something has changed ('nothing is drawn unless there is somebody to see it and something to see'); support.ts:112-115 clamps device pixel ratio to 2 ('the number of pixels drawn is capped rather than believed'). The scene is built economically — scene.test.ts 'the board is built out of two meshes and not sixty-four', 'the marks are reused rather than rebuilt, however many times they change' — and the flat board is always available as the lightweight view. Frames drawn are counted and reported honestly through Diagnostic (support.ts:118-128, shown in Board3DArea.tsx:125-132). | Nothing measures the frame rate and nothing degrades in response. There is no level-of-detail reduction (no detail or segment parameter anywhere in three/pieces.ts), no simpler-material fallback, and no automatic switch to 2D on poor performance — the switch exists only for an outright graphics failure. The sole timing test measures processor cost, not frame rate, and is load-sensitive: scene.test.ts 'the three things that happen while somebody is playing all cost well under a frame' failed for me when run alongside three other files on this loaded machine and passed when the file was run alone (18/18). |
| 270 | Provide graceful 3D fallback | When the graphics context fails, the device lacks support, or resources cannot be allocated, say so clearly and offer the flat board, preserving the current position, variation tree, recoverable engine search state and unsaved annotations. | done | no | three/support.ts:15-72 enumerates every failure — no WebGL, library would not load, context lost, build threw — and returns one plain sentence per case plus an honest `retryable` flag, with 'asking for the flat board is not a failure'. Board3D.tsx renders the real `Board` with the same props on the flat route (:700-712), so the position, variation, highlights and annotations are untouched by construction; the engine runs in another process entirely. Tests pass: support.test.ts 'every way the 3D board can fail falls back to the flat one and says so plainly', 'trying again is offered only where it could work', 'a 3D board that works says nothing either'; scene.test.ts 'a scene that cannot be built does not leave half of one behind'. | — |

### Accessibility And Input System — 271 to 280

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 271 | Establish accessibility requirements for the board | Make squares and pieces identifiable through assistive technology with meaningful labels and state descriptions, and make sure selection, legal-move highlighting, check status and move completion are never signalled by colour alone. | partial | yes | What works, on the board a person actually uses: Board.tsx:122-164 gives the board role=grid with rows and gridcells, names it with whose turn it is, and gives every square its own description — 'e4, white pawn' plus 'picked up', 'can move here' or 'last move' (:109-118). Selection, candidate captures, legal destinations, the previous move, marked squares and the keyboard cursor are all shape- or border-coded, not colour-coded: board.css:44-64 (a dot via ::before, inset rings, a dashed or two-tone outline). The project keeps its own honest audit of this, rendered on screen and held by a passing test: settings.ts:1266-1320 BOARD_ACCESS_NOTES, shown in settings/ShortcutHelp.tsx:123-130, tested by settings.test.ts 'every note about the board says what was found, and every gap says what would fix it' (58/58 pass). | Check status is colour alone, which the section names explicitly. `.cw-sq.is-check` sets only a background — board.css:62 and chessSettings.css:162 — with no ring, shape or icon, and Board.tsx's describe() at :112-116 omits check from the square's accessible name, so a screen reader is never told. A marked square's gold ring is likewise absent from the description. Beyond that, a passing test certifies three more gaps: settings.test.ts:775-778 asserts the board still has no aria-activedescendant (so nothing says where the cursor is), no aria-live (so move completion is silent), and keeps `disabled={!interactive}` on its squares, which means a finished or watched game cannot be read square by square at all. |
| 272 | Implement keyboard-based move entry | Let a person walk the squares and choose a piece and destination with the keyboard alone, with predictable focus movement, a visible focus indicator, respect for legal-move validation, no accidental triggering of unrelated ORBIS shortcuts, and a way to cancel a half-finished move. | partial | yes | Board.tsx:93-107 takes the keyboard when interactive, walks a cursor with the arrow keys, picks up and puts down with Enter or Space, and lets go with Escape; every arrow calls preventDefault so the page does not scroll and no other shortcut fires, and ChessView.tsx:148-158 skips its own '?' handler while anything is being typed into. Only rules-legal squares are offered (choose() at :80-91). The cursor is kept visible, and settings.ts:105-107,121 makes the strong two-tone ring the default precisely because the board's own dashed accent outline measures about 1.6:1. The wiring is held by a passing test in both directions — settings.test.ts:731-743 "the board's own keys in this map are the keys Board.tsx really answers" reads Board.tsx and checks every key in BOARD_KEYS is answered and every key answered is documented (58/58 pass) — and the equivalent logic is exercised behaviourally for the 3D board by selection.test.ts 'Enter does exactly what clicking where the cursor is does', 'walking the squares goes the way the screen goes, not the way White goes', 'walking stops at the edge rather than falling off it'. | A promotion cannot be completed by keyboard alone, so the acceptance criterion fails for pawn promotions: the chooser (Board.tsx:166-179) is a dialog the keyboard is never moved into, it does not hold focus, and Escape still goes to the board and only releases the piece — leaving the chooser open over a board with nothing picked up. A passing test certifies it: settings.test.ts:778 asserts Board.tsx still has no focus trap. No test exercises the 2D board's key handling behaviourally; settings.test.ts:731 inspects the source rather than pressing a key. |
| 273 | Implement accessible move announcements | Announce the board and game-state changes that matter to assistive technology — selected piece, legal destination, move played, check, checkmate, promotion prompt, engine-analysis completion — concisely and without narrating every frame or low-level update. | partial | yes | The behaviour is fully written for the 3D board, which has a polite live region fed by a spoken description (three/Board3D.tsx:739 and selection.ts:163-178 describeSquare, which does include 'in check'), tested by selection.test.ts 'a square says what is on it and what is going on there' and 'the king in check is the king the rules say is in check'. Reachable announcements elsewhere are narrower: the Play screen's role=status line says whose move it is (arena/ArenaGame.tsx:703-710), the puzzle solver gives polite feedback (puzzles/Solver.tsx:211), and the engine says when it is ready (analysis/EnginePanel.tsx, AnalysisStudio.tsx:178). | The board a person uses announces nothing, and a passing test certifies it: settings.test.ts:776 asserts `!board.includes('aria-live')`. The project's own audit records it as missing — settings.ts:1294-1298, 'picking a piece up, playing a move, check and checkmate are all silent' — and notes that whose turn it is sits in the board's own name, which readers announce unreliably when it changes under them. The Study screen prints 'White to move, in check' in a plain span with no live region (analysis/AnalysisStudio.tsx:307-311), and the Play screen's status line never mentions check at all. The one complete implementation, Board3D's live region, is in a component nothing mounts. |
| 274 | Support coordinate and notation preferences | Let a person configure coordinate visibility and notation, apply the notation choice consistently to the board, the move list, exported content where relevant and the training interface, and keep the internal move model independent of the display format. | partial | yes | Coordinates are done properly: settings.ts:33,117 define the setting, settings.ts:895 publishes it as a document attribute, and chessSettings.css:145 hides the labels everywhere in one rule, so it reaches boards the settings screen does not own; per-theme label colours are tested at 4.5:1 by settings.test.ts 'the square names can be read on the square they sit on' (58/58 pass). The notation function itself is correct and well tested — settings.ts:973-980 notateSan, with settings.test.ts:596-608 covering that figurine converts only a leading piece letter (so 'bxc6' stays a pawn move and 'Bxb5' becomes a bishop), and that UCI falls back to the readable form rather than inventing coordinates. The internal model is untouched: stored moves keep both SAN and UCI (settings.ts:969-971). | The notation preference reaches nothing but the settings screen's own preview. notateSan has exactly two call sites, both inside settings/ChessSettings.tsx (:560 an engine line, :598 a sample move row). The real move lists render raw SAN — analysis/MoveTree.tsx:50,67 in the Study screen and arena/ArenaGame.tsx:868-869 on the Play screen — and nothing in the academy, puzzle or endgame screens calls it either. So choosing figurine or UCI visibly changes only the settings panel, not the board, the move lists or the training interface. |
| 275 | Provide colour-vision-friendly themes | Offer board themes that stay distinguishable for different colour-vision needs, with contrast between light and dark squares, readable move highlights and a clear selected square — and never encode the difference between legal, illegal, previous and candidate moves by colour alone. | partial | yes | Five themes including an explicit High contrast board of pure white and near-black with a heavy outline on every piece (settings.ts:248-262), chosen in Chess World's Settings area. The contrast guarantees are measured by luminance — the measure that survives colour-vision deficiency — and tested, passing (58/58): settings.test.ts 'every theme has a visible difference between its two squares' (at least 2:1), 'every piece can be seen on both of its squares, by its colour or by its outline' (3:1 by fill or collar, against the light square, the dark square and the check square), 'the square names can be read on the square they sit on' (4.5:1). Shape coding is present for most states: board.css:51-64 marks a legal destination with a dot, a candidate capture and the selected square with inset rings, a marked square with a gold ring, and the keyboard cursor with an outline that chessSettings.css:186-195 upgrades to a black-and-white double ring by default. | Two of the four states the section names are still colour alone. The previous move is a translucent cyan wash across the whole square with no shape or border (board.css:44-50), and the king in check is a background colour in every theme (board.css:62, chessSettings.css:162). Nothing tests the shape coding either — every colour test measures contrast ratios only, so deleting the legal-move dot or the selection ring would leave all 58 tests passing. Secondarily, no theme is designed against a named colour-vision deficiency: Forest pairs a red mark (#c7462f) and red check (#9c3b2e) with green squares, which passes the luminance test but has no hue-separation check behind it. |
| 276 | Support reduced motion | Honour the computer's "reduce motion" setting and offer an in-app one, cutting decorative movement while still showing what a move did. | partial | yes | src/renderer/src/components/chess/board.css:115 gates the square transition on prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference; same pattern at editor/editor.css:305 and settings/chessSettings.css:373; coach/coach.css:377 kills a bar transition under reduce. In-app setting board.highlightFade (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:424-428) with :root[data-cw-fade='off'] .cw-sq { transition: none } at settings/chessSettings.css:210. three/Board3D.tsx:78-91 watches the media query live and three/support.ts:95,102 zero both easings. Test: src/renderer/src/components/chess/three/support.test.ts 'a machine that has asked for less movement gets none' — ran it, 8/8 pass. | Two transitions on screens a person can reach ignore the preference completely: the Study screen's evaluation bar (analysis/analysis.css:98, transition: height 160ms) and the Find-a-position progress bar (search/search.css:120, transition: width 120ms). Nothing tests the 2D board's reduced-motion gate — only the 3D durations are tested. |
| 277 | Support text scaling and layout adaptation | Keep controls, labels, move lists and settings usable when text is scaled or the app is zoomed, with nothing important clipped and long names wrapping or truncating with the full value still reachable. | partial | yes | Container queries in 19 chess stylesheets measure the screen rather than the window: chess.css:77 reflows the fifteen-item rail into a sideways-scrolling strip under 760px (nothing becomes unreachable), plus library.css:329, analysis.css:299 and :308, settings/chessSettings.css:367, editor.css:300. Long names wrap (live/live.css:142,275 and analysis.css:201 use overflow-wrap: anywhere) or ellipsise (library.css:216-227, openings.css:134,245), and the row button's accessible name carries the whole title (library/GameRow.tsx:43,46). | There is no application zoom at all — no setZoomFactor and no zoom item in the native menu anywhere in src/main/index.ts — and the type scale is fixed pixels (src/renderer/src/styles/tokens.generated.css:7-9), so a system text-only scaling change does nothing. A truncated player or opening name has no title tooltip, so a sighted person has no way to see the full value, only a screen reader. No test covers scaling or clipping. |
| 278 | Define a consistent keyboard-shortcut registry | Register Chess World's keys centrally, check them against the keys Orbis and Orion already own, make them context-sensitive so they do not fire while you are typing, and never quietly take over an existing global key. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/settings.ts:1107-1197 is a real central map (CHESS_SHORTCUTS, BOARD_KEYS, APP_SHORTCUTS) with nested scopes (992-1019) and conflictsIn (1215) separating a same-place clash from a nested-scope decision. Tests in src/shared/chess/settings.test.ts — ran it, 58/58 pass — include 'no key is claimed twice in the same place', "Chess World claims no chord the rest of Orbis already has", 'the app chords in this map are really in the files it says they are', and "the board's own keys in this map are the keys Board.tsx really answers". The sheet is reachable: ? opens it (ChessView.tsx:148-158 → settings/ShortcutHelp.tsx), and that handler steps aside for inputs and contenteditable. | The registry is registered to nothing. Fifteen of the sixteen Chess World chords declare owner: 'ChessSettings.tsx' and that file has no key handler at all; a repo-wide grep finds no screen-level handler for ArrowLeft/ArrowRight/Home/End/F/A/Shift+A/N/S/1/2/3/4/Shift+S anywhere under src/renderer/src/components/chess. Only ? works. Four entries still describe a four-tab screen that no longer exists ("The Play tab", "Study", "Library", "Live" on 1-4). No test checks a CHESS_SHORTCUTS entry against its stated owner, which is the one check that would have caught fifteen dead keys. |
| 279 | Provide customisable shortcuts safely | Let people change Chess World's keys and put them back, explain which action already owns a combination they pick, keep their choices in the profile, and validate stored key definitions so a corrupt file cannot kill unrelated typing. | not started | no | The parts that would support it exist and are tested: normalizeChord/chordFromEvent/matchesChord (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:1044-1083) and conflictsIn/describeConflict (1215-1245), with ALL_SHORTCUTS frozen as data. | Nothing lets anybody change a key. There is no editing UI in settings/ChessSettings.tsx or settings/ShortcutHelp.tsx, no restore-defaults for keys, no conflict explanation against a combination a person chose, and no storage for one — the saved settings shape (settings.ts:88-94) has no shortcut field, so there is nothing to validate on load either. |
| 280 | Conduct accessibility regression testing | Test keyboard navigation, focus, board announcements, colour contrast, reduced motion, text scaling and key conflicts as repeatable tests in the release process, documenting any limit the desktop framework imposes. | partial | — | src/shared/chess/settings.test.ts is in the release suite (scripts/testFiles.mjs:258, run by npm test) and I ran it: 58/58 pass. It covers colour contrast for every theme on both square colours, piece-against-square visibility by fill or outline, coordinate legibility, the strong cursor's two bands, the panel's own colours on its background; key conflicts; the key map asserted against the real Board.tsx and App.tsx; and BOARD_ACCESS_NOTES held to naming both what works and what does not, with 'the two gaps the notes make the most of are still really there' asserting against Board.tsx that aria-activedescendant and aria-live are still absent and the squares are still disabled. | Keyboard navigation and focus are only checked by grepping Board.tsx for e.key comparisons — nothing runs the board, so focus order, the promotion chooser's trap and Escape are untested. No test covers reduced motion on the 2D board, text scaling or clipping. The board's announcements are recorded as absent (settings.ts:1282-1299) rather than fixed, so the suite currently locks the absence in place. |

### Personalisation, Settings And User Control — 281 to 290

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 281 | Build a unified Chess World settings system | One settings screen covering board look, notation, sound, animation, analysis defaults, training preferences, storage information and engine options, grouped with explanations, where every released switch really changes behaviour. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/settings.ts holds 30 fields in 5 described groups, each with a reach saying where its value lands, and drawnAs() (329) makes the screen draw a control only for a value that goes somewhere. settings/ChessSettings.tsx is mounted at the 'settings' area of ChessView.tsx and uses the real Board for its preview, the real analysisRequestFrom for its engine ask, and the real sound player. Tests pass (58/58): 'a field that goes nowhere yet says so, and says what will read it', 'every board attribute the component sets is one the stylesheet acts on', 'every board setting that claims to go nowhere else really has no other route', 'every field says what it does, and nothing is left undescribed'. | Two of the eight categories named are absent: no training preferences and no storage information. One of the five honest "nothing reads this" statements is now false — analysis.evalBar says "Nothing draws the bar yet" (settings.ts:539) while the Study screen draws it (analysis/AnalysisStudio.tsx:288, analysis/EvalGraph.tsx:16). And the stylesheet that gives every board setting its effect — the :root[data-cw-*] rules in settings/chessSettings.css — is imported only by the two lazily loaded settings modules, so a saved board theme, piece style, piece size or cursor has no matching CSS after a restart until someone opens Settings or presses ? once in that session. |
| 282 | Separate global and session-specific settings | Tell preferences that apply everywhere apart from settings that belong to one game, training session or analysis job, make the difference clear on screen, and stop a session change leaking into unrelated saved sessions. | partial | no | SESSION_OVERRIDABLE (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:781-799) names the seventeen fields one game may disagree about and states why the rest belong to the person; ChessSessionOverride, effectiveChessSettings (829) and IgnoredOverride report a refused override in words instead of dropping it. Tests pass (58/58): "a game's override wins for its own field and for nothing else", 'a field that belongs to the person cannot be overridden by one game', 'every overridable field exists, and the ones held back are held back for a stated reason', 'no override means the preference exactly'. | Nothing in the running app uses it. effectiveChessSettings has no caller outside settings.test.ts, no screen creates or stores a session override, and ChessSettings.tsx shows all thirty fields with no scope marked, so the distinction the section asks to make clear in the UI is made nowhere a person can see. |
| 283 | Define settings precedence | Document and apply one precedence order where global preferences, session overrides and engine defaults meet, resolve the effective value before the operation starts, and show the configuration in force when it changes the result. | partial | no | The rule is written down and implemented per field in effectiveChessSettings (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:821-867): a session override beats the global for its own field and for nothing else — not its group, not the file — and cannot push a value past the range the preference would have been held to. Tests pass (58/58): 'an override is held to the same range the preference would have been', 'an override of the wrong kind is refused rather than coerced', 'an override that agrees with the preference is not called an override'. describeEngineRequest (933) states what the engine will be asked in words, and the settings screen prints it before the button is pressed. | No operation resolves an effective value: the resolver has no caller outside the test, so nothing is resolved before a search starts and no screen shows the configuration in force for a session. Engine defaults are the third party in the order and are only described field by field (threads and hash as statements, settings.ts:507-516); the three-way order appears nowhere in docs/chess-world.md. |

Tests pass (src/shared/chess/settings.test.ts, ran it, 58/58): 'resetting a group touches nothing outside it' and 'every default is a value its own field would accept'. | — |
| 285 | Persist board and layout preferences | Keep board orientation, theme, coordinate visibility, panel layout and notation in the active profile, validate them on load, and fall back safely on an invalid value rather than failing to open. | partial | yes | settings/chessSettingsStore.tsx saves all five groups under one key and re-applies the board ones to the document element at module load (applyStoredChessBoardSettings, lines 75 and 153, also called from ChessView.tsx), wrapping every read and write so blocked storage cannot take the screen down and saying so with canSave. readChessSettings (src/shared/chess/settings.ts:669) validates field by field, keeps every good value in a file with one bad one, and reports each fallback as a readable sentence that ChessSettings.tsx:263-293 shows. Tests pass (58/58): 'one bad value costs one preference, never the file', 'a number outside its range is held to it and the holding is reported', 'a choice that is not one of the choices falls back and the choices are named', 'settings that are not settings at all fall back wholesale, and say so', 'a reading can be written back and read again unchanged'. | Three of the named preferences are stored and not honoured: board orientation (arena/ArenaGame.tsx:139 and analysis/AnalysisStudio.tsx:64 each hardcode useState<'w'\|'b'>('w')), notation (notateSan has no caller outside the settings screen) and panel layout (nothing persists one) — the first two are declared honestly in NOT_EVERYWHERE (settings.ts:575-580). They are kept in localStorage, which chessSettingsStore.tsx:18-30 says belongs to the installation rather than the profile, while a profile-aware chess-settings.json already exists in the main process unused. And the :root[data-cw-*] rules sit in the lazily loaded settings/chessSettings.css, so a restored board theme has no effect until the settings panel or the key sheet is opened once. |
