Tests passing in src/shared/chess/analysis.test.ts: 'a position that was not analysed is a gap in the graph and an unnamed move in the list', 'nothing is drawn across a gap: the graph comes back as separate runs', 'what is recorded from an answer carries the position, and a bound is marked as unsettled', and the case asserting "a number calculated elsewhere is not shown here". | The graph is recomputed in full on every new evaluation point — `graphSegments` and `graphGaps` are memoised on the whole `points` array (EvalGraph.tsx:43-44), so each arriving evaluation rebuilds every run and gap rather than updating incrementally, which is the first thing the section asks for. And the click-to-move mapping has no test: EvalGraph.tsx:47-53 turns a click x into `Math.round(fraction * span)` with `span = points.length - 1`, while the gap rectangles are placed with `(gap.to + 1) / span` — two different conventions for the same axis, and nothing checks that a click on a given stretch lands on the move drawn there. |
| 466 | Animation and Reduced-Motion Behaviour | Keep animations from blocking actions or getting in the way of fast move navigation, honour a reduced-motion preference, let board transitions be reduced, keep dialogs usable without elaborate transitions, make loading indicators mean real work, never let animation leave the screen obscured, and never queue up animations under rapid navigation. | done | yes | Chess World has eight transition declarations across its thirteen stylesheets and no `@keyframes` at all, and every one is inside a reduced-motion guard: board.css:115 (`no-preference` only), coach/coach.css:377 (`reduce` turns the bar fill off), editor/editor.css:305, settings/chessSettings.css:373. The 3D board reads `matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)')` with a live change listener and drops both its durations to zero (three/Board3D.tsx:82-115, 435, 603; three/support.ts:95-104). Test passing: 'a machine that has asked for less movement gets none' in src/renderer/src/components/chess/three/support.test.ts (8/8 pass). Nothing can queue animations — there are only CSS transitions, which replace themselves — nothing overlays the interface, dialogs carry no transitions, and the loading indicators name real work (ChessView.tsx:260 "Opening Library…" while the chunk loads; ReportPanel's sweep counter showing done of total). Colours and radii come from the ORBIS tokens (var(--cyan), var(--line), var(--faint), var(--radius-sm)). | — |
| 467 | Keyboard and Screen-Reader Accessibility | Make the core chess work reachable without a mouse: keyboard navigation, focus indicators, named buttons, labelled controls, announcements for important changes, keyboard promotion, accessible dialogs, an alternative way through the move list, a text description of the position, and accessible status for live freshness and analysis progress — without announcing every engine update. | partial | yes | The 2D board is a focusable `role="grid"` of `role="gridcell"` buttons: arrow keys walk the squares, Enter or Space picks a piece up and puts it down, Escape lets go (Board.tsx:93-107,122-128). Each square carries a written description — the square, the piece and its colour, whether it is picked up, whether it can be moved to, whether it was the last move (Board.tsx:109-118) — which is the text representation of the position the section asks for. The promotion question is a `role="dialog"` with named buttons (Board.tsx:167-172). There are 71 `role="status"` regions across the area, with `aria-live="polite"` on the lesson block, the commentary list, the puzzle verdict, the settings read-back and the 3D board's spoken line. Every rail item has a visible always-on label and `aria-current` (ChessView.tsx:242-250), deliberately unlike the browser's icon-only rail. Focus rings are explicit (`:focus-visible` in chess.css:62). | Nothing here is tested: there is not one component test in src/renderer/src/components/chess outside three/'s pure helpers, so no keyboard or assistive-technology workflow is exercised anywhere. Three concrete holes: the grid keeps focus on itself and sets `tabIndex={-1}` on every cell with no `aria-activedescendant` (Board.tsx:127,153), so a screen reader never reads the square the cursor is on — the per-square labels are unreachable by keyboard; the promotion dialog has no `aria-modal`, no focus move into it and no focus trap; and the engine's status is a plain `role="status"` (EnginePanel.tsx:58) that rewrites as state changes with no rate limiting, which is the announcement flood the section warns against. |
| 468 | Responsive Layout and Window Constraints | Work at narrow and wide windows, on high-DPI screens and under different OS scaling, when the window is resized during a game, with side panels open, in analysis layouts and split-pane library views, keeping keyboard focus sane across layout changes and never letting an essential control end up permanently out of reach. | partial | yes | Built on container queries rather than media queries, which is the right question when Chess World sits in a ~600 px column beside the chat: `.cw-view { container-type: inline-size; container-name: cw-screen }` (chess.css:2) with nineteen breakpoints across thirteen stylesheets, and five areas declaring their own containers (cw-lb, cw-live-area, cw-searchbox, cw-cfg-panel, cw-ed-screen). At ≤760 px the rail turns from a column into a sideways-scrolling row and drops its one-line descriptions, never its names, so no control disappears (chess.css:72-95). High-DPI is handled for the 3D board by capping the draw scale at 2× and clamping nonsense values, and the viewport is only taken when it is a real size — both tested and passing: 'the number of pixels drawn is capped rather than believed' and 'the viewport is only taken when it is a real size' (three/support.test.ts, three/scene.test.ts). | Nothing tests the 2D layout at any width — the container queries have never been exercised or photographed, and docs/chess-world.md:303 still records that no runtime check of this screen exists. Nothing covers operating-system display scaling, resizing during an active game, or keyboard focus surviving the rail's switch from column to row. The high-DPI handling is the 3D board's only; the 2D board and every panel draw at whatever the window gives them. |
| 469 | Performance Regression Benchmarks | Repeatable automated checks on the operations that matter — PGN parse time, opening a stored game, query latency, library render, move navigation, memory after repeated navigation, worker startup, cache lookup, evaluation-graph cost — with broad stable thresholds that will not fail because a shared machine is busy, and trend comparison where a strict threshold cannot work. | partial | — | One real regression check exists, passing, and it is built exactly as the section describes — broad thresholds, a warm-up round, and its reasoning written out: 'the three things that happen while somebody is playing all cost well under a frame' in src/renderer/src/components/chess/three/scene.test.ts:347-396 (200/200/400 rounds asserting under 8 ms, 4 ms and 1 ms, plus the relation that turning the camera must cost less than placing every piece). Both files are in the suite list (scripts/testFiles.mjs), so these run under `npm test`. | Two of the nine listed operations are covered. src/shared/chess/library.test.ts's 'a library of several thousand games filters, sorts and pages quickly' times eleven operations over 5,000 games but asserts only correctness — it prints the numbers and says so deliberately ("The numbers printed here are what the machine running the test actually did, not a target"), so it cannot detect a regression. Nothing measures PGN parse time, opening a stored game, database query latency, library rendering time, move-navigation responsiveness, memory use after repeated navigation, worker startup, or cache lookup, and nothing anywhere does trend comparison. |
| 470 | Renderer and Accessibility Acceptance Gate | Verify the twelve items together: board interaction under frequent engine updates, large library scrolling, large PGN viewing, graph accuracy, memory stability over repeated navigation, reduced motion, keyboard navigation, accessible dialogs, high-DPI rendering, narrow-window usability, profile switching, and performance with analysis and live updates running at once. | partial | — | Four of the twelve have a passing test. Evaluation graph accuracy: 'a position that was not analysed is a gap in the graph and an unnamed move in the list' and 'nothing is drawn across a gap: the graph comes back as separate runs' (src/shared/chess/analysis.test.ts). Reduced motion: 'a machine that has asked for less movement gets none' (three/support.test.ts). High-DPI rendering: 'the number of pixels drawn is capped rather than believed' (three/support.test.ts). Profile switching (for a pending write): 'lands a save queued before a profile switch in the profile that made it' (src/main/chess/data.test.ts). | Eight of the twelve are unverified, and three of those the code would not pass. Board interaction under frequent engine updates: never measured, and nothing is memoised so every engine line reconciles the board (462). Memory stability over repeated navigation: no test. Accessible dialog behaviour: no `aria-modal`, no focus trap, untested (467). Performance under simultaneous analysis and live updates: never attempted. Large library scrolling, large PGN viewing, keyboard navigation and narrow-window usability are each implemented but tested only at the data or pure-helper layer, never in a mounted view — there is no component test in the chess renderer tree outside three/. There is no gate artefact: nothing records a run of the twelve, and no script under scripts/ photographs or probes Chess World. |

### Logging, Diagnostics, And Support Workflows — 471 to 480

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 471 | Structured Chess World Logging | Record important subsystem events as structured entries with timestamp, subsystem, event type, severity, request or job id, engine or provider id, error category, duration and retry count, using ORBIS's existing logging, instead of ad hoc console messages — and keep raw engine protocol output behind an explicit diagnostic setting. | partial | yes | One structured event type exists and is well made: `LiveDiagnostic { provider, operation, kind, message, field?, status?, at }` with a nine-value `DiagnosticKind` — http, rate-limit, schema, network, timeout, cancelled, circuit-open, pgn, refused (src/shared/chess/live.ts:41-63). It is raised by the adapter and the manager, pushed on `chess:live-diagnostic` (src/main/index.ts:4350), read back field by field by `readDiagnostic` (liveView.ts:406+), filtered by `worthShowing`, and shown on the Live screen (LiveChess.tsx:292-298, 434-438). Tested and passing: 'a listing fault reaches the port as the same diagnostic, not as an empty list' (lichess.test.ts), 'a cancelled request is not worth putting in front of anybody: it is what switching rounds does' (liveView.test.ts). And the ad hoc scatter the section forbids is genuinely absent: there is not one `console.log`, `console.warn` or `console.error` anywhere in src/shared/chess, src/main/chess or the renderer chess tree. | Live is the only subsystem with structured events. The engine's `onLog` hook (engine.ts:262, called at :836 with the line truncated to 400 characters) is passed by nothing in src/main/index.ts:4341 — only engine.test.ts:213 supplies a no-op — so raw UCI protocol logging is a hole with no sink and no diagnostic setting behind it. Imports, exports, storage refusals and analysis requests emit no events at all. No event carries severity, duration, a request or job id, or a retry count. ORBIS's own structured per-profile bounded log (src/main/securityLog.ts) is for security events only, with a shape that cannot hold anything else, and chess writes nothing to it — so an import, an analysis request and a live-feed failure cannot be correlated, which is the acceptance criterion. |
| 472 | Error Taxonomy and User-Facing Messages | One consistent set of error categories — bad position, bad PGN, unsupported variant, engine unavailable or broken, analysis cancelled, storage unavailable, corruption, migration failure, import conflict, export failure, feed unavailable, rate limit, timeout, stale data, unsupported source, resource limit, profile switch failure — each with a message saying what happened and what the person can do, no stack traces as the primary experience, and the technical detail kept in diagnostics. | partial | yes | The live side is the model: `faultWords` turns each diagnostic kind into a different next step — wait for a rate limit, wait for an open circuit, "this is a fault in Orbis, not a tournament being off" for a changed shape, "there is nothing at that address any more" for a 404 — and `LiveFault` shows the provider's own reason verbatim then what happens now, withholding the Try again button during a cooling-off period (src/shared/chess/liveView.ts:1064-1086; live/LiveFault.tsx:21-33). Tested and passing: 'a rate limit, an open circuit and a changed shape are three different things to tell somebody' (liveView.test.ts). Elsewhere: `ChessEngineError` carries eleven codes and a `recoverable` flag (engine.ts:78-97); `data.ts`'s refusal says a file could not be read, nothing was overwritten, and to try again in a moment (data.ts:57); the Academy record refuses a file written by a newer Orbis and names both version numbers (shapes.ts:390-391); ChessView.tsx:128 strips Electron's "Error invoking remote method" wrapper and distinguishes "you have no games" from "your games could not be read". Tests passing: 'an install record that cannot be read is a problem, not a missing engine' (engine.test.ts), 'an import summary reports the zeroes that matter' (library.test.ts). | There is no one taxonomy. The categories live in three unrelated unions — `ChessEngineError['code']` (11 values), `DiagnosticKind` (9 values) and `InstallError` — plus dozens of ad hoc `{ ok: false, reason }` strings across src/shared/chess, so nothing can map a failure to a category across subsystems or decide what recovery to offer. Three of the listed categories reach nobody: a failed engine install is silent, because `chess:engine-failed` is sent from src/main/chess/ipc.ts:269 and no preload listener for it exists (src/preload/index.ts has no `onChessEngineFailed`); an export failure arrives as the raw Node error from `writeFile` (ipc.ts:159); and a profile-switch failure only reaches `console.error('[personal] could not switch profile')` at src/main/index.ts:4363. And there is no local diagnostics store to preserve the technical detail in (473). |
| 473 | Diagnostic Snapshot | A local snapshot that can be generated on demand carrying versions, OS, subsystem status, schema version, engine identity, recent errors, cache statistics, job status, provider connection, resource usage, migration status and feature flags — with no PGNs, annotations, credentials or personal file contents by default, its contents explained before sharing, and redaction controls. | partial | yes | One slice is built properly and reachable at Tools → Reference data (ChessView.tsx:223-224): src/shared/chess/reference.ts reports which datasets are really on this computer under the rule that "a dataset is here because something looked, not because a setting says where it would be" — a path-only piece of evidence can never produce "present", it produces "nobody has looked" with the path shown (reference.ts:8-13). `probeOpenings` asks the opening dataset a question and counts the answer; `engineEvidence` reads the engine's real state, binary path and installed version and fills in nothing the engine did not send, with an unrecognised state becoming "not known" rather than being bent into one (reference.ts:451-481). Reached with the raw status as `unknown` so the full record arrives at runtime (search/ReferencePanel.tsx:55-61). Tests passing in src/shared/chess/reference.test.ts: 'the engine's state is read as the engine reports it, and installed is not the same as running', 'a field the engine did not send is not filled in', 'an answer the panel does not understand is reported as not known, never bent into a state', 'the summary counts each state apart and does not dress a fault up as a score'. | There is no snapshot. Nothing gathers one, nothing generates one on demand, nothing explains its contents before sharing, and there are no redaction controls — and there is no channel for it (docs/ipc-mapping.md:110-112 lists no diagnostics channel). Eleven of the thirteen listed contents are absent: ORBIS and Chess World versions, operating-system version, the stored-data schema version (only the Academy record has one, `PROGRESS_VERSION`), last error summaries, cache statistics, job status, the live provider's connection status, resource-usage estimates, migration status, enabled feature flags and recent redacted error events. The privacy half of the requirement is met only because there is nothing to leak. |
| 474 | Engine Diagnostics | A way to inspect engine health that tells the stages apart — binary found, executable, process started, UCI handshake done, ready, options accepted, search started, completed, cancelled, failed, process exited unexpectedly — shows the detected engine identity and configuration, never calls an engine ready just because its executable exists, and never reports a successful search without a valid accepted result. | partial | yes | The service distinguishes nearly every stage and refuses every shortcut. `EngineStateName` is not-installed \| installing \| stopped \| starting \| handshaking \| ready \| searching \| stopping \| failed (engine.ts:68-76), and `ready` is reached only after `uciok`, the option list, the options Orbis sets being accepted and `readyok` — "a binary existing on disk is not an engine" (engine.ts:51-55). `EngineStatus` carries the engine's own name and author as it said them in this process, the installed release tag, `verified`, `binaryPath`, `binaryFrom`, the advertised options, what was actually applied after clamping, the current `searching { requestId, fen, startedAt }`, `queued`, `lastError`, `recoverable`, and `problem` for something that is there and cannot be used, never reported as "not installed" (engine.ts:265-290). All of it tested and passing in src/main/chess/engine.test.ts (39/39): 'nothing is ready until the engine has said hello, and then it says what it is', 'an engine that never answers "uci" is a failed start, not a wait', 'an engine that answers "uci" and then never "readyok" does not reach ready either', 'an engine that dies during the handshake is a failure, not a request left searching', 'an engine that dies mid-search fails the search with what it said, and can be started again', 'an install record that cannot be read is a problem, not a missing engine', 'an engine somebody chose is used, and a bad choice is reported rather than swapped for another'. | There is no diagnostic view, because the bridge truncates the status before it reaches any screen: `chessEngineStatus` is declared as `{ state: string; detail: string; name?: string; version?: string }` (src/shared/types.ts:2358), so no panel can show the advertised options, the applied values, `problem`, `lastError`, the queue or the running request. `version` is not even a field of `EngineStatus` — the real name is `installedVersion` — so EnginePanel.tsx:61's `${name} ${version}` always renders the name alone. Search started, completed, cancelled and failed are surfaced nowhere: the only engine text on screen is one `role="status"` line (EnginePanel.tsx:58-65), plus the single-line evidence on the Reference data screen, which reads the raw object as `unknown` and so does see state, path and version but shows no stages. And a failed install reaches nobody at all — `chess:engine-failed` is sent (ipc.ts:269) with no preload listener. |
| 475 | Live-Feed Diagnostics | Internal diagnostics for live-game ingestion — connection and subscription status, last successful message, last source timestamp, last local receipt, processing latency, sequence gaps, reconnection attempts, rate-limit responses, parsing failures, snapshot-recovery attempts, current staleness, provider warnings — marking anything the provider does not give as unavailable rather than inventing it, and separating the provider's publication delay from Orbis's own processing delay. | partial | yes | The source-delay versus local-delay distinction is made explicitly on screen: "last move arrived X ago · Orbis last checked Y ago", with the reason written beside it — "A screen showing only the second looks healthy while the feed is dead" (live/LiveViewer.tsx:144-151). Connection status is one of five named states passed through unaltered, and an unrecognised one is read as `connecting` rather than trusted (liveView.ts:316-320; test 'a connection state that is not one of the five is read as connecting rather than trusted'). Subscription and generation are reported (manager.ts:418-422). Staleness is a four-value freshness recomputed against now with the provider's declared embargo, and `delayed` is not treated as a fault (live.ts:68-69). `health()` really counts games, generation, late-dropped updates, batches, payloads and pending (manager.ts:426-434). Unavailable is marked, not invented: a clock with no moment attached is left out rather than pinned to now, a game of unknown age is never called live, and a provider that sends no clocks is reported as not sending them (LiveViewer.tsx:182). Rate limits, parsing failures and refusals arrive as typed diagnostics with a next step (LiveChess.tsx:434-438). Tests passing: 'the counters add up, for when somebody reports that the board froze', 'an older snapshot arriving late is noted and does not rewind the board', 'a repeated delivery is recognised by its id and reported as a duplicate', 'a game missed while disconnected is recovered from the next snapshot, and only the new moves count' (manager.test.ts); 'a broadcast the provider holds back can never be called live, however promptly it arrives', 'a game nothing is known about the age of says so rather than being called live' (liveView.test.ts); 'a dropped stream is retried, with a bounded jittered wait, and then given up on' (lichess.test.ts). | Three of the thirteen tracked fields do not exist and one is unreachable. Processing latency is measured nowhere — nothing times the gap between receipt and the record being updated. Reconnection attempts are counted only inside the stream loop and put into a state detail string (lichess.ts:915-922), never into `health()` or any view, so the count is lost once the message is replaced. Snapshot-recovery attempts are not counted at all. And `pending` — the only backlog signal there is — sits in `health()` and is never displayed; the window shows only `lateDropped` (LiveChess.tsx:439). So of the four causes the acceptance criterion names, a developer can reach provider delay, network interruption and parsing failure, but not a local processing backlog. There is no diagnostics view either: what exists is user-facing warning text on the Live screen, not a panel gathering these fields. |
| 476 | Support Bundle and Export Policy | Let a person build an optional support bundle of redacted diagnostics on their own computer — showing which categories of data go in, letting them review or redact, excluding credentials and their full private game library by default, writing to a clear path, reporting success or failure honestly, and never uploading itself. | not started | no | Nothing in the tree builds a bundle. A grep for support-bundle wording across src/ matches only src/main/tabLockStore.ts (an unrelated phrase). The only chess export paths are src/main/chess/ipc.ts (`chess:export-pgn`, `chess:to-pgn` — one game or many, that the person names) and the `chess` category in src/shared/portable/registry.ts:250-271, which exports all six profile files including the whole games library. | There is no support bundle at all: no channel, no screen, no category list, no redaction step, no destination path, no completion report. The nearest thing that exists — the profile export category — does the opposite of what this section asks, because it carries the entire private chess library by design. |
| 477 | Operational Health Dashboard | Give a developer somewhere to see which chess subsystems are working and which have degraded — the database, the rules service, the engine, the job scheduler, the analysis cache, live providers, PGN import/export, profile ownership, diagnostics and storage — kept off the main landing page and showing states somebody can act on rather than a row of green ticks. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/reference.ts:103-191 and src/renderer/src/components/chess/search/ReferencePanel.tsx are a real health view for four datasets, reachable at ChessView.tsx:223 under Tools → 'Reference data', off the landing area. Every row carries the evidence that produced its state and `summarise` (reference.ts:508) refuses to reduce it to one score; a path from a setting can never read as present (reference.ts:283). src/shared/chess/reference.test.ts passes 18/18 (confirmed), including 'a setting that points at a path NEVER makes a dataset present' and 'installed is not the same as running'. The engine's live state also appears in arena/ArenaGame.tsx:947 and settings/ChessSettings.tsx, and an unreadable games file is reported at ChessView.tsx:126-128. | Only the engine and the datasets have a state anywhere. Seven of the ten named subsystems are absent: no rules-service check, no background-job scheduler (none exists), no analysis cache (none exists), no PGN import/export subsystem state, no profile-ownership display, no diagnostics state, no storage-availability indicator. The `refused` and `mayWrite` answers the data store already produces (src/main/chess/data.ts, src/main/storage/jsonFile.ts `mayWrite()`) reach no screen. There is no single view: the states are scattered across three areas, and nothing tests the assembly. |
| 478 | Recovery Instructions | Tell a person what to actually do when something fails — install or pick an engine, retry an interrupted import, restore a backup, reconnect to a live source, rebuild a disposable cache, repair the local database, restart a failed job — with steps that match the real implementation and never 'delete your profile folder'. | partial | yes | src/shared/chess/liveView.ts:1064-1086 (`faultWords`) gives a distinct next step per fault kind — a rate limit says Orbis is waiting, an open circuit says it has stopped asking, a changed shape says this is Orbis's fault and retrying will not help, a network fault says check you are online, a 404 says it is gone — and live/LiveFault.tsx:30 withholds the retry control while `wait` is true, so no control is offered that would make things worse. src/shared/chess/liveView.test.ts passes 55/55 (confirmed), including 'a rate limit, an open circuit and a changed shape are three different things to tell somebody'. For the engine: reference.ts:133 states how it is fetched and ReferencePanel offers 'Set it up now'; ArenaGame.tsx:947-955 explains Stockfish is separate and offers to fetch it. For an unreadable data file, src/main/chess/data.ts moves it aside and says so (data.test.ts passes 25/25, confirmed). Nothing anywhere tells a person to delete a profile. | Only two of the nine named failures have guidance. There is no recovery path for an interrupted import, no backup to restore, no analysis cache to rebuild, no database repair or migration route, no background-job restart, and no 'export your recoverable data before repairing' step — because none of those subsystems exists in the chess tree. Nothing collects the guidance that does exist into one troubleshooting place. |
| 479 | Observability Without Excessive Overhead | Keep diagnostics from becoming the performance problem: bound the logs, sample repetitive events, set a retention policy, never write a large entry for every engine node or live update, keep critical errors even when verbose logging is off, and measure what the collection costs. | partial | — | The restraint is real and deliberate. src/main/chess/engine.ts:434 logs only UCI lines it does not recognise — `info` lines, the repetitive ones, are handled at engine.ts:438 and never logged; engine.ts:836 caps every line at 400 characters; engine.ts:359 caps a stderr line at 300. live/LiveChess.tsx:296 keeps at most four diagnostics (`.slice(0, 4)`) and drops cancellations as noise; library/ImportSheet.tsx:120-125 prints at most 40 warnings and then a count. src/main/chess/live/lichess.ts retries with a bounded jittered wait and opens a circuit after repeated failures (lichess.test.ts: 'a dropped stream is retried, with a bounded jittered wait, and then given up on'). | There is no log sink at all, so none of this is observable. `ChessEngine`'s `onLog` hook (engine.ts:262) is supplied in exactly one place — engine.test.ts:213 — and src/main/index.ts:4341 constructs the engine without it, so every engine diagnostic line is computed and then discarded. No retention policy, no verbose/essential split (so there is nothing to disable and nothing to preserve), and no measurement of diagnostic overhead anywhere. |
| 480 | Diagnostics Acceptance Gate | Confirm ten things before calling diagnostics finished: engine failures can be diagnosed, import errors carry context, database errors are recorded safely, live-feed staleness is visible, job failures can be correlated, reports are redacted, support bundles exclude private chess data, logs are bounded, recovery guidance is accurate, and a failure in diagnostics does not crash the app. | partial | — | Six of the ten hold with passing tests. Engine failures: `EngineStatus` carries state, detail, lastError, problem and recoverable (engine.ts:808-813), with engine.test.ts covering a crash mid-search, a failed handshake and a search that times out. Import context: library/ImportSheet.tsx:85-128 shows imported, skipped duplicates, failed games with reasons, and warnings. Database errors: src/main/chess/data.test.ts passes 25/25 (confirmed) — a file that cannot be read is never written over and the save says so. Live staleness: liveView.test.ts passes 55/55 (confirmed). Logs bounded: see Section 479. Diagnostics not crashing the app: engine.test.ts 'an update listener that throws does not take the search down with it'. | Four of the ten cannot be met: there is no job scheduler, so no job failure to correlate; there are no diagnostic reports, so nothing to redact; there is no support bundle, so nothing to exclude private data from. And one confirmed hole in the first item: src/main/chess/ipc.ts:269 sends `chess:engine-failed` and nothing subscribes — src/preload/index.ts:784-789 bridges seven push channels and not that one — so a failed engine install is silent in the window, which is the single failure most likely to need diagnosing. |

### Comprehensive Testing And Regression Prevention — 481 to 490

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 481 | Requirement Traceability Matrix | Produce a final matrix covering all 500 sections, each classified as implemented-and-verified, implemented-but-unverified, partial, not implemented, blocked by a documented dependency, or not applicable with a technical reason — naming for each one the implementation location, the verification method, the observed result, known limitations and remaining work, and never marking something implemented just because a matching filename exists. | partial | — | docs/chess-world-ledger.md is a real matrix: it covers 001-500 exactly once in ten parts, carries an Evidence column naming files and tests, has a separate 'On screen' column, errs toward not-implemented where nothing was found, and states its own method so it can be redone. docs/chess-world.md is the architecture it traces. | It was compiled without the spec text — it says so itself in 'Two honest limits on this ledger' — so its per-section subjects are inferred from the 85 section-number citations in the code rather than from what each section asks, and 281 of the 500 rows are an admitted ceiling on what is missing rather than a measurement. It is already wrong in three checkable places: ChessView.tsx now mounts fifteen areas (it records that nothing is mounted), trainingView.test.ts passes 70/70 (confirmed — it records three failures), and 47 chess test files are now named in scripts/testFiles.mjs (it records several as orphaned). The 'blocked by a documented dependency' class is absent, nothing is recorded as not-applicable with a reason, and no script or test in the repo keeps the matrix matching the code — there is no ledger guard in scripts/. |
| 482 | Unit-Test Coverage | Have meaningful unit tests for the critical pure logic — notation conversion, position and move validation, game identity, PGN normalisation, FEN canonicalisation, analysis-cache keys, evaluation orientation, mate scores, duplicate detection, import validation, metadata normalisation, job-state transitions, retry policies, resource limits, live-event deduplication, staleness and migration-version selection — with fixtures from independently verified cases and boundary and invalid inputs tested, rather than chasing a coverage number. | partial | — | 32 test files sit in src/shared/chess and 5 under src/main/chess, all named in scripts/testFiles.mjs; src/shared/testSuite.test.ts passes 3/3 (confirmed), which is what makes a written test a run test. Of the eighteen named areas, thirteen have a test: notation, position and move validation and FEN building in rules.test.ts; game identity in library.test.ts:515 (two records compared by `fingerprint`); evaluation orientation and mate-score representation in engine.test.ts:489 ('a mate score is a mate, and it is turned round for the side to move exactly once') and settings.test.ts (58/58 pass, confirmed); duplicate detection in library.test.ts; retry policies in lichess.test.ts (429 honoured, bounded jittered retry, circuit opened and closed); resource limits in engine.test.ts:231/248/267 ('limits are read defensively, and nonsense does not become an unbounded search'); live deduplication in live.test.ts; staleness in liveView.test.ts (55/55 pass, confirmed); engine job-state transitions in engine.test.ts:825. | Five areas are untested. PGN normalisation has no test of its own — src/shared/chess/pgn.ts has no .test.ts, and `readPgn` is exercised only for variant tags in variants.test.ts:159-188; `writePgn` and the round trip are never checked. Import validation has no test — src/main/chess/store.ts, the only games store, and src/main/chess/ipc.ts, the only channel checker, both have no test file. Metadata normalisation is covered only indirectly through library.test.ts. Analysis-cache keys and migration-version selection have nothing to test, because neither an analysis cache nor a migration exists in the chess tree. |
| 483 | Integration-Test Coverage | Test whole service interactions using real local components — PGN to canonical game to database to library screen, FEN into the studio, an analysis request through Stockfish and back, engine output into the evaluation graph, a provider response into live state and onto the board, a puzzle attempt into a saved progress record, an academy assessment into a saved completion, backup to restore, a profile switch into profile-specific storage, a cache lookup into reuse, and a cancellation into released resources — mocking only genuinely external things, and never the component under test. | partial | — | Five of the fourteen flows have a real integration test that passes. Profile switch to profile-specific storage: src/main/chess/data.test.ts drives ChessDataStore against a real temporary filesystem and passes 25/25 (confirmed), including 'lands a save queued before a profile switch in the profile that made it' and 'shows the other profile its own record, and never the first one's'. Analysis request to engine and back: src/main/chess/engine.test.ts drives a scripted child process that speaks UCI end to end. Provider response to canonical live state: lichess.test.ts and manager.test.ts run recorded NDJSON and PGN responses through the adapter. Cancellation to resource cleanup: engine.test.ts:606/627/711 ('a cancelled search fails as cancelled, and the engine is usable again straight after'). Puzzle attempt and academy assessment to a saved record: src/renderer/src/components/chess/keeping.test.ts passes 20/20 (confirmed) and covers the read/save/coalesce path that puzzles/store.tsx:89-114 and academy/storage.tsx:92-111 use over `chess:save-training` and `chess:save-academy`. | Nine flows have no test. PGN parser to canonical model to database is untested at the seam that matters — store.ts has no test file. Database to library screen, FEN input to Analysis Studio, engine output to the evaluation graph, and live state to the visible board are all untested because no test in this tree renders a React component: there is not one .test.tsx anywhere under src. Backup to restore and cache lookup to reuse cannot be tested — neither subsystem exists. And nothing joins the pieces: keeping.test.ts tests the renderer helper against injected functions, data.test.ts tests the main-process store directly, and no test runs a value from a screen through the preload bridge to the disk and back. |
| 484 | End-to-End User Journeys | Build end-to-end tests for five journeys through the real integrated application: playing Stockfish and reopening the saved game, importing a PGN and analysing, annotating, exporting and reimporting it, following a live game through a disconnection and recovery, keeping two local profiles' private records apart, and completing an academy lesson and finding the progress restored. | not started | — | No end-to-end harness exists. package.json has no Playwright, Puppeteer, Spectron, testing-library or jsdom dependency and no e2e script; there is no .test.tsx under src; there is no CI directory. docs/chess-world.md states it in its own known limitations: 'Not one line of this has been seen running.' The journeys' pieces are all mounted — ChessView.tsx:186-228 mounts ArenaGame, AnalysisStudio, GameLibrary (with ImportSheet), Academy and LiveChess — but nothing walks any of them. | All five journeys are untested end to end, and two cannot pass as written even by hand. Journey B step 7 ('save supported annotations') fails: AnalysisStudio.tsx:25-28 keeps the whole study in memory and never writes it back, and StoredGame (model.ts:67-85) has no field for a variation tree or notes — the comment at model.ts:73 says analysis lines live in a study, and there is no study store and no `chess:save-study` channel among the 31 documented in docs/ipc-mapping.md. | 485 | Failure-Injection Testing | Deliberately inject nineteen controlled failures — from a missing Stockfish binary and a crashed engine process through an invalid PGN, a corrupted database, a failed migration, a network timeout, provider rate limiting, duplicate and out-of-order live events, a profile switch during active work, to an unexpected shutdown — and for each one verify the failure is detected, represented accurately, that data is protected, resources released, recovery offered where appropriate, that the UI does not claim success, that diagnostics are useful, and that everything else still works. | partial | — | Thirteen of the nineteen are injected by a test. src/main/chess/engine.test.ts covers a missing binary ('with nothing installed the state says so, and an analysis is refused honestly'), an invalid configuration ('a bad choice is reported rather than swapped for another'), a process crash ('an engine that dies mid-search fails the search with what it said, and can be started again'), malformed UCI output and a malformed bestmove, stale results after a cancel ('results that arrive after a cancel are dropped, and never land on the next position'), and worker termination. src/main/chess/live/lichess.test.ts covers a network timeout, a network failure, a 429 on both request and stream, a refusal inside a stream body, a dropped stream, and a changed response shape. src/shared/chess/live.test.ts covers a re-sent snapshot and a corrected line. src/main/chess/data.test.ts passes 25/25 (confirmed) and covers a corrupted file, a file that cannot be read at all, a profile switch during an in-flight save, and a save queued microseconds before the process ends. | Six are not injected. Invalid PGN is only exercised for variant tags in variants.test.ts — the store's import path has no test at all. Insufficient storage and an interrupted export are never simulated. A failed migration, a failed backup and an invalid restore file have no implementation to fail, so they cannot be tested until that machinery exists. None of the covered cases is verified at the UI layer — the 'the UI does not falsely report success' check is unexercised everywhere, because no component is tested. |
| 486 | Regression Protection for Existing ORBIS | Prove Chess World was purely additive — that the browser, Orion, navigation, shortcuts, profiles, settings, local data, downloads, tabs, themes and background services all still work — by running the existing suite and smoke tests and reviewing every modified global style, shared store, navigation definition and entry point, and by not fixing unrelated behaviour along the way. | partial | — | The integration points really are additive. `git diff` across the shared files deletes two lines in total: one in src/main/index.ts (the `personalStores` array, re-added with chessStore and chessData) and one in src/main/knowledge/ipc.ts. The one shared-code change of substance, src/main/storage/jsonFile.ts, is additive — a new optional `what` option plus `mayWrite()` and `flushSync()` — and it is regression-tested: 58 lines were added to src/main/storage/jsonFile.test.ts, which passes 17/17 (confirmed). Shortcuts are guarded by construction: src/shared/chess/settings.test.ts passes 58/58 (confirmed), including 'no key is claimed twice in the same place' and 'the app chords in this map are really in the files it says they are', which reads Board.tsx and ipc.ts as text so the map cannot drift from the code. src/main/ipcMapping.test.ts passes 6/6 (confirmed): no channel is registered in two places and every chess channel is documented. | The suite has not been run to completion against this tree (no record of a full run exists and running it was out of scope here), there is no startup smoke test, and there is no CI to run either. Two files in the same uncommitted batch are not chess at all — src/renderer/src/app/useChat.ts (+5) and src/renderer/src/components/MemoryOffer.tsx (+42), with a matching line changed in src/main/knowledge/ipc.ts — so unrelated chat and memory behaviour changed alongside the chess work, which is exactly what this section forbids unless it is required and justified, and nothing in the tree justifies it. |
| 487 | Test Data and Fixture Integrity | Keep a deliberate test-data strategy with the fixture kinds separated — unit, integration, performance, manually verified regression games, malformed input, provider responses and engine protocol — never ship test games as a person's own library, never mix invented provider responses with real records, label demonstration data, keep fixture licensing compatible, and never present a fabricated game as authentic or historical. | partial | — | The honesty rules are implemented and tested. src/shared/chess/endgamesData.ts gives every entry a `provenance` sentence saying it was authored for Orbis and not taken from a game (endgamesData.ts:39/51/62/73), and src/shared/chess/endgamesData.test.ts passes 7/7 (confirmed), including 'the provenance says where each one comes from and never claims a game it is not from' and 'the claims that say "in n" are proved by search'. src/shared/chess/famous.ts records why the fifteen games are moves-as-data with commentary written for Orbis, replayed through rules.ts by famous.test.ts. openingsData.ts records its upstream repository, commit, fetch date, entry count and CC0-1.0 licence, checked against the repository rather than assumed (reference.test.ts 'the opening dataset carries the licence that was actually checked', 18/18 pass, confirmed). | 488 | Automated Quality Gates | Configure the project's available automated checks — formatting, linting, type checking, unit and integration tests, build validation, dependency auditing, package verification, migration tests, a desktop startup smoke test and the critical journeys — reusing the existing conventions, and state plainly which checks are mandatory before release and which are informational, so a failed mandatory check is never reported as a passed gate. | partial | — | Chess is inside every gate the project has. package.json defines `typecheck` (three tsconfig projects), `test` (scripts/runTests.mjs over the explicit list), `notices` and `dist` (typecheck → stage:browser → notices → build → electron-builder --win). 47 chess test files are named in scripts/testFiles.mjs, and src/shared/testSuite.test.ts passes 3/3 (confirmed) — 'every test file on disk is one npm test runs' — which is the guard that stops a written chess test from going unrun. src/main/ipcMapping.test.ts passes 6/6 (confirmed) and gates the channel documentation, channel naming and double registration. scripts/runTests.mjs sets a non-zero exit code on any failure, so a failing run cannot read as a pass. | There is no formatting or linting tool in the project at all — no eslint or prettier in package.json and no lint script — so those two checks cannot be configured without introducing tooling. There is no CI of any kind (no .github and no other CI configuration), so nothing runs these gates except by hand. No dependency audit step, no migration tests, no desktop startup smoke test, no end-to-end journeys. And nothing anywhere states which checks are mandatory before release and which are informational. |
| 489 | Manual Acceptance and Usability Review | Actually use the built desktop application and review twenty-one things — first entry, moving between modules, the board, creating a game, picking an engine, analysis controls, PGN import and export, live discovery, the library, puzzles, academy progression, error messages, loading and empty states, cancellation, profile switching, settings persistence, resizing, keyboard navigation, reduced motion and recovery after restart — inspecting the console and logs, and recording what remains. | not started | — | Nothing in the tree records a pass over the running product for Chess World. docs/ holds screenshot directories for other areas (views1-shots through views7-shots, branding-audit-shots, parity) and nothing for chess; a search for any chess image under the repository returns only the three chess .md files. No runtime check over the browser debugging port, no console-error review, no notes on resizing, keyboard navigation, reduced motion or recovery after restart. docs/chess-world.md says it outright in limitation 11: 'Not one line of this has been seen running.' | The whole section is unperformed. The one piece of adjacent real work is honest rather than substituting for it: BOARD_ACCESS_NOTES in src/shared/chess/settings.ts and settings/ShortcutHelp.tsx record what does not work for a keyboard user, but they are findings read off Board.tsx, not a review of the running application — and settings.test.ts's 'the two gaps the notes make the most of are still really there' confirms they are still open. |
| 490 | Final Testing Acceptance Gate | Before calling testing complete, run every required check, fix the regressions the new work caused, re-run the checks, verify the most important journeys, local persistence, engine-result authenticity, live-feed failure handling, profile isolation, and packaging and startup — then produce a concise, evidence-based test report that never reports a test as passing when it was not run. | not started | — | No test report exists anywhere in the repository. Of the ten steps, the ones I could check are unmet: packaging and startup have never been verified with Chess World in them — the newest installer, dist/Orbis-Setup-0.3.0.exe, is dated 30 September 2026, while the first chess commit (5373cf82) is 16 October 2026, so no packaged build has ever contained the feature; engine-result authenticity has never been verified against the real binary, which docs/chess-world.md states in 'The stand-in is not the engine'; the live-feed steps have been verified only against recorded fixtures, which this specification's own Section 499 says cannot prove provider availability. | Nine of the ten steps are unevidenced and no report of any kind was produced. Individual files do pass — I confirmed testSuite 3/3, ipcMapping 6/6, data 25/25, jsonFile 17/17, reference 18/18, liveView 55/55, settings 58/58, endgamesData 7/7, trainingView 70/70, keeping 20/20 — but a set of files run one at a time by an auditor is not the gate this section describes, and nothing in the tree records even that much. |

### Release Engineering, Packaging, And Final Acceptance — 491 to 500

| # | Section | What it asks | Status | On screen | Evidence | What is missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 491 | Production Build and Packaging Integration | Fold Chess World into the existing production build so the packaged application has what it needs — assets included, services bundled, migrations present, default configuration valid, engine integration available, worker scripts packaged, resource paths working outside development mode, permissions right and existing resources untouched — without relying on a developer's own repository layout. | partial | — | The build does include it. out/renderer/assets holds a compiled chunk and stylesheet for every lazily loaded area — ChessView, ArenaGame, PuzzleWorkspace, EndgameLab, Academy, OpeningLab, FamousGames, CoachPanel, GameLibrary, AnalysisStudio, PositionSearch, PositionEditor, Board3DArea, LiveChess, ReferencePanel, ChessSettings and ShortcutHelp — dated 16 October 18:25, i.e. after the ChessView rewrite, and electron-builder.yml ships `files: out/**`, so they would be packaged. There are no development-only absolute paths: src/main/index.ts:4326 and :4341 root the store and the engine at `app.getPath('userData')`, `chessHome` is `join(userData, 'chess')` (src/main/chess/ipc.ts:321), `ORBIS_CHESS_ENGINE_HOME` is the only override, and src/main/chess/install.ts:61 falls back to `%APPDATA%`. No worker scripts and no migrations need packaging. | No packaged build has ever contained Chess World — the only installer in dist/ predates the feature by two weeks — so nothing has verified resource paths, runtime permissions or startup outside development mode, which is precisely the gap this section warns about ('a source file being present in the repository' does not mean it is in the packaged app). electron-builder.yml adds no chess entry of any kind and no smoke test was added for it. |
| 492 | Stockfish Distribution and Licensing | Treat Stockfish as a separately governed dependency: decide and document whether it is bundled, downloaded, supplied by an existing mechanism or selected from a local install; verify its licence obligations, version attribution, source-availability and redistribution conditions against the actual binary; include the required notices; and keep the application working and honest when the engine is absent. | partial | yes | The model is chosen, reasoned and documented: not bundled, fetched from upstream's own GitHub release on request into <userData>/chess, because Stockfish is GPL-3.0-or-later and Orbis ships no GPL code — src/shared/chess/reference.ts:122-146 states the source repository, the licence by name and id, and that the engine is not part of the Orbis download; docs/chess-world.md 'The engine: why nothing is bundled' states the same with the consequences; electron-builder.yml carries no engine. src/shared/chess/reference.test.ts passes 18/18 (confirmed), including 'every dataset names itself, its source, where it would live and what works without it'. The licence travels with the binary rather than being written from elsewhere — install.test.ts 'only the program and its licence come out of the archive, out of everything in it' and 'a missing licence is reported rather than invented' — and the download is hashed against the release's published digest, with a digest-less release refused unless explicitly accepted (install.test.ts). The missing-engine state is honest and on screen: engine.test.ts 'with nothing installed the state says so, and an analysis is refused honestly', arena/ArenaGame.tsx:947-955 names Stockfish as a separate free program under its own licence and offers to fetch it rather than showing a fake opponent, and ReferencePanel lists the licence and what still works without an engine. | The arrangement has never been exercised against the real release. install.test.ts proves the code's behaviour against synthetic archives and recorded release JSON; no test in this tree has fetched or run an actual Stockfish build, which docs/chess-world.md states ('no test in the repository has ever run the real binary'). So the recorded version attribution, digest and Copying.txt handling are unverified against the real distribution — exactly what this section means by 'do not claim licensing compliance without verifying the actual binary and distribution arrangement'. Nothing surfaces the engine's licence in the application's own third-party notices either; resources/notices.json is generated from node_modules only. |
| 493 | Third-Party Dependency Governance | Review every dependency Chess World introduced or updated — why it is needed, whether ORBIS already had the capability, maintenance status, licence, security, bundle size, native requirements, upgrade implications and whether it is optional — avoid duplicate libraries, pin to the repository's policy, do not upgrade unrelated packages, run available vulnerability checks, and document any unavoidable risk. | partial | — | Chess World added exactly one dependency: `chess.js: ^1.4.0` (package.json:102), and `git log -S '"chess.js"' -- package.json` shows it arriving in the chess commit 5373cf82 and nowhere else — no unrelated package was upgraded with it. Its licence is recorded (BSD-2-Clause, docs/chess-world.md 'Three outside pieces') and swept into resources/notices.json by scripts/generate-notices.mjs, which walks the installed tree so it cannot drift from what is really there. The 'does ORBIS already have this' question is answered where it arose: three/Board3D.tsx:50-52 states three.js is used 'Because it was already here', and `git log -S '"three"'` confirms it arrived with an earlier non-chess commit. No duplicate rules engine was added — src/shared/chess/rules.ts is the only opinion on legality and ten modules import it. | There is no vulnerability check anywhere in the project: no `npm audit` step, no audit script, no CI to run one. And the review itself is only half recorded — the docs table carries licence and whether the package is bundled, and nothing records chess.js's maintenance status, bundle-size impact, native runtime requirements, upgrade implications, or whether it is optional or mandatory. |
| 494 | Upgrade Safety and Backward Compatibility | Test upgrading a representative existing ORBIS installation and show it loses nothing — no deleted user data, no reset settings, no replaced profiles, no lost browser state, no broken shortcuts, no overwritten user files and no reinitialising as a fresh install — migrating Chess World data only when needed, staying compatible with supported earlier schemas, and offering a safe recovery path when an old installation cannot be upgraded automatically. | partial | — | The additive shape is what makes it safe, and much of it is tested. Chess World only adds files — six per-profile JSON files registered at src/shared/portable/registry.ts:264-271 with schemaVersion 1 — and never rewrites an existing one. src/main/chess/data.test.ts passes 25/25 (confirmed) and covers the existing-installation case directly: 'answers with the defaults on a first run, and says nothing is wrong'; a file written by a newer Orbis is moved aside rather than having its unrecognised parts dropped; 'a field this version has never heard of survives a trip out and back, so a stricter reader cannot delete somebody's data'; and a file that could not be read is never written over, even on the way out. The one-time move of the three screens' old window-storage copies into the profile is itself tested — src/renderer/src/components/chess/keeping.test.ts passes 20/20 (confirmed), including 'leaves a profile that already holds something alone', 'writes nothing over a profile file that could not be read, however empty it looks', and 'takes the record back when the save was refused, so the next launch tries again'. No existing setting or shortcut is reset (settings.test.ts 58/58, confirmed). | No upgrade has been performed from a packaged installation, because no packaged build contains the feature (the newest installer predates it by two weeks). There is no migration machinery for chess data, so there is no test of an older Chess World schema being brought forward, and no safe recovery path is defined for an installation that cannot be upgraded. The six files are also untested as a set through a real profile switch in the running application — only the store is tested, in isolation. |
| 495 | First-Run Experience and Feature Discovery | Fit Chess World into ORBIS's own navigation and visual system with a discoverable entry point, and on first entry tell a person what they can actually do here — play the engine, analyse a game, import a PGN, solve puzzles, learn, explore openings, follow live games, review saved games — showing only what is genuinely available, explaining a missing engine or an unavailable provider instead of faking either, and not redesigning anything else. | partial | yes | The entry point is integrated and discoverable: `chess` is a capability in src/shared/commandCenter.ts:292 with a plain description ('Play the engine, study your games, train, and follow live tournaments') and eleven keywords, so the launcher and Ctrl+K find it, and src/renderer/src/App.tsx:36/1110 mounts ChessView lazily inside the existing shell. ChessView.tsx:59-95 names all fifteen areas in four groups — Play, Learn, Your games, Tools — each with a one-line blurb kept always visible rather than behind a hover (the comment at :53-58 says why), which is the activity list this section asks for. Prerequisites are honest where they bite: ArenaGame.tsx:947-955 explains the engine is a separate free program and offers to fetch it rather than showing a playable fake; LiveChess.tsx shows only what a provider really answered; ReferencePanel says which datasets are here and what still works without each. | There is no first-entry experience at all: ChessView.tsx:101-109 opens straight onto Play with no welcome, no introduction to the activities, and no distinction between a first visit and a return. The rail presents all fifteen areas identically whether or not their prerequisites are met — an engineless machine still sees Play, Study and Coach offered the same way as Library. And nothing tests any of this, since no component in the chess tree has a test. |
| 496 | Release Configuration and Feature Readiness | Make a deliberate release-readiness configuration that distinguishes fully available features from those needing an engine, those needing an external provider, those that only work for compatible data, experimental ones and intentionally deferred ones — without exposing internal development flags as user settings, without advertising a feature as complete because a route exists, without silently enabling unsupported variants, without disabling existing ORBIS functionality, and documenting what is deferred and why. | partial | yes | A real readiness mechanism exists at field level and it is the right shape. src/shared/chess/settings.ts:326-330 makes each field declare where its value lands, and `drawnAs()` prints a statement instead of drawing a control for the four fields that are only stored — each naming what has to change for it to work (settings.ts:457, 535, 539, 543) — so a dead switch cannot ship. src/shared/chess/settings.test.ts passes 58/58 (confirmed), including 'every board setting that claims to go nowhere else really has no other route'. The same honesty covers capability: variants.ts answers in four yes-or-no questions what Orbis can really do with each variant and says no to everything but standard chess; reference.ts:273-281 returns `not-supported` — 'Orbis cannot use this yet' — rather than inviting somebody to install a tablebase that nothing reads. No internal development flag is exposed as a user setting. | There is no release configuration for the feature as a whole. Nothing anywhere distinguishes fully available from engine-dependent from provider-dependent from deferred, and nothing records the deferred items and the reason. The fifteen rail areas are presented alike although they are not alike: Analysis Studio offers notes and variations and can persist neither (AnalysisStudio.tsx:25-28, model.ts:67-85), Board in 3D needs a graphics context it may not get, and Live depends entirely on one external provider — none of which the configuration says. |
| 497 | Release Notes and User Documentation | Write accurate documentation for what was actually delivered — core capabilities, engine requirements, PGN and FEN, local storage and profile isolation, backup and restore, analysis behaviour, live provider limitations, privacy and network behaviour, troubleshooting, unsupported formats and known limitations — describing real behaviour rather than intentions, never claiming every game is available or every feed instant, and keeping engine results distinguishable from generated prose. | partial | — | docs/chess-world.md is a substantial and mostly honest engineering document covering much of the list: capabilities module by module, the engine requirement and why nothing is bundled, PGN and FEN handling, per-profile local storage with the file paths, analysis behaviour, the live provider's four shape constraints and its freshness limits, the three outside pieces with their licences, eleven known limitations in the order they would bite, and a section separating what is verified from what is not. It keeps the distinction this section insists on: commentary.ts 'may only say what rules.ts or a real engine evaluation established… Every remark carries the facts it leaned on.' In the product, settings/ShortcutHelp.tsx documents every key Chess World answers plus the board access findings that are still true. | There is no user-facing documentation and no release notes. README.md does not mention chess at all. Backup and restore, privacy and network behaviour, and troubleshooting for a person rather than a developer are uncovered. Worse, the architecture note now describes a different program from the one in the tree: it states the screen is 'the original four-tab view with eight imports, and not one of them is any of those' and that every panel written for Chess World 'is reachable by nothing', while ChessView.tsx mounts fifteen areas including all of them — which is the hypothetical-architecture documentation this section forbids. Nothing keeps the file matching the code, unlike docs/ipc-mapping.md, which has ipcMapping.test.ts. |
| 498 | Final Product Audit and Defect Triage | Audit every major subsystem against Sections 001-500 — does it exist, is it integrated, does its core path work, is data persisted, are errors handled, are resources cleaned up, is profile ownership enforced, do tests exist, do they pass, is it in the packaged build — then classify defects by severity and resolve the release-blocking ones, without hiding a defect by removing its UI or quietly lowering a requirement. | partial | — | An audit document exists — docs/chess-world-ledger.md, covering 001-500 with per-section evidence and a separate on-screen column — and docs/chess-world.md lists eleven known limitations ordered by how much they would hurt, which is an informal severity ordering. Several of the audit's own questions are genuinely answerable from tests I confirmed: resource cleanup (engine.test.ts cancellation and restart), error handling (data.test.ts 25/25), profile ownership for the six stored files (data.test.ts, keeping.test.ts 20/20). | The audit was compiled without the specification text, by its own admission, and is already wrong in three checkable places (see Section 481), so it is guesswork where this section wants evidence. There is no severity classification and no defect triage at all — no list of defects, no severities, no release-blocking set. And at least two items on this section's own release-blocking list are present and unrecorded: 'core workflows that cannot complete despite being presented as available' applies to Analysis Studio, which offers notes and variations on screen and can persist neither (AnalysisStudio.tsx:25-28; StoredGame has no field for them, model.ts:67-85; no `chess:save-study` channel exists among the 31 in docs/ipc-mapping.md); and the packaged-build question is unanswerable because no package has ever contained the feature. |
| 499 | Complete End-to-End Release Verification | Build and exercise the final integrated application through the repository's real release process — twenty-five steps from applying the changes to an existing installation, packaging, launching the package, opening Chess World and playing real Stockfish, importing and analysing a real PGN, saving and reopening the analysis, exporting and checking the PGN, opening a FEN, browsing the library, testing a live source and a provider-unavailable state, switching profiles, checking persistence, backup and restore and cancellation, reviewing diagnostics and launching without development services — recording the operating system, build configuration and engine version, and distinguishing a fixture test from a real-provider verification. | not started | — | None of the twenty-five steps has been performed for Chess World and no record of any of them exists. The only production package in the tree, dist/Orbis-Setup-0.3.0.exe, is dated 30 September 2026 against a first chess commit of 16 October 2026, so steps 3-6 have never happened with the feature in them. No real Stockfish search has ever run in this tree (docs/chess-world.md), no real PGN has been imported through the running application, no live source has been exercised against the live provider, and no operating system, build configuration or engine version is recorded anywhere. The only release-shaped evidence is the renderer build in out/renderer/assets — every chess chunk compiled on 16 October at 18:25 — which this section explicitly says does not substitute: 'a successful development launch does not prove that the packaged build works.' | The entire verification is unperformed. Steps 21 (backup and restore) and 22 (cancelling a long-running job) cannot be performed at all, because neither a chess backup/restore path nor a background-job system exists; step 11 (save and reopen the analysis) cannot succeed, because the analysis session is never persisted. |
| 500 | FINAL MASTER ACCEPTANCE, DELIVERY, AND NON-NEGOTIABLE COMPLETION STANDARD | Pass the final acceptance in seven parts — the product capabilities, chess correctness, engine authenticity, live data, data integrity, the untouched host application and quality — then deliver ten real artifacts (traceability, architecture, database and migration, engine integration, live provider, local data and privacy, testing, known issues, release readiness and user documentation) and a completion report that classifies the implementation, names the verification outcomes, states the data-safety results and the known limitations, and ends in a justified release recommendation. | partial | — | Parts B, D and F are genuinely strong and tested. B (chess correctness): rules.ts is the only opinion on legality and ten modules import it, with rules.test.ts covering castling from the rights, en passant from the square, mate, stalemate, insufficient material and repetition from the history; variants.ts refuses to read a Chess960 game as standard chess (variants.test.ts); endgamesData.test.ts passes 7/7 (confirmed), re-deriving every 'mate in n' claim by exhaustive search. D (live data): lichess.test.ts, live.test.ts, manager.test.ts and liveView.test.ts (55/55, confirmed) cover documented endpoints, deduplication, corrections, staleness, received-versus-worked-out clocks, rate limits and a changed schema. F (host application): the integration deletes two lines in total across the shared files, settings.test.ts (58/58, confirmed) guards the existing shortcuts, and ipcMapping.test.ts (6/6, confirmed) guards the channel boundary. Parts of E hold too: data.test.ts (25/25) and keeping.test.ts (20/20) show games, preferences, puzzles, the repertoire, the Academy record and the plan surviving restarts, profile switches and unreadable files. Of the ten deliverables, 1, 2, 4, 5, 6 and 8 exist in docs/chess-world.md and docs/chess-world-ledger.md. | The acceptance has not been passed and the final report does not exist. C (engine authenticity) is proved only against a scripted stand-in — no real Stockfish has ever run here. E fails on two counts: user annotations and analysis results are not persisted at all, because the study session is in-memory and StoredGame has no field for it. G fails on integration tests for most flows, on end-to-end coverage (none), on race conditions and performance (never measured), on accessibility of the running product (never checked) and on packaging (never tested). Four deliverables are entirely absent — 3 (database and migration documentation), 7 (testing report), 9 (release-readiness report) and 10 (user-facing documentation) — and the two that exist are stale or guessed. There is no completion report in the K format and no release recommendation; on the evidence here the honest one would be 'not ready for release'. |
